# What the published performance figures were measured against, earlier.
#
# The older half of docs/measured-figures.txt, moved when that file passed
# the megabyte a file in this repository may be. Nothing here is read by
# the suite; the fingerprint groups all stayed behind. It is kept because
# a measurement that was worth recording is worth keeping, and several of
# these entries are the reason a later one was not attempted twice.

# 2026-08-17, the shape behind the sweep's totals, which it now prints: nine
# architectures, fifteen formats and five shapes, with 864 of the 24939
# sequences run on a device. A total says nothing about what it covers, and
# what it covers is the whole of the claim the number is quoted for.
#
# The gate is bounded as a whole as well as stage by stage -- 1500 s against
# the twelve minutes it takes -- because four stages each inside their own
# bound still add to a run nobody notices growing, which is exactly how half
# an hour became an hour.

# 2026-08-17, what the reference's remaining time is. With one load a
# fixture, its 397 s divides into 274.7 s decoding each fixture into
# binary64 and 122.7 s computing with it -- so decoding is sixty-nine per
# cent of the reference and about forty-five per cent of the whole sweep,
# and it is already one pass a fixture. That is the floor of having a second
# implementation in a wider format, and it is where it should be rather than
# somewhere nobody looked.
#
# The gate holds each stage to roughly twice what it took here and fails
# when one exceeds it -- repository checks 30 s, conformance 1250 s,
# fixtures 200 s, fuzzing 10 s. Twice, because a busy machine takes half
# again as long and that is not news, while a stage that doubles has had
# something added to it. The gate went from half an hour to an hour once and
# nothing said so until somebody timed it by hand.

# 2026-08-17, the sweep loaded the reference four times a fixture. Splitting
# its cost by sequence is what found it: the four sequences cost 281, 289,
# 310 and 333 seconds across a sweep though they are one, two, five and eight
# tokens long. Costs that flat under an eightfold difference in work are not
# the work -- they are the load, which is every matrix of the fixture decoded
# into binary64, done once for each sequence asked about.
#
# One load a fixture now, running all four. The sweep went from 1431 s to
# 599 s, and the reference within it from 1213 s to 386 s, on the same
# figures to the digit: 24939 sequences, none outside tolerance, none
# unlearned. The gate is about eleven minutes where it was twenty-five.
#
# The parallelism I had written down as the next thing is not needed for
# this, and would have bought a quarter of what a second glance at the
# measurement did.

# 2026-08-17, where the sweep's twenty-four minutes go, which it now says:
#
#   fixtures built          59.9 s    4 per cent
#   the reference         1206.9 s   85 per cent
#   the engine             ~157   s   11 per cent
#
# The independent implementation is eighty-five per cent of the sweep, and
# the sweep is ninety-four per cent of the gate: four fifths of every gate
# run is one binary64 transformer computing what a binary32 one has already
# computed. That is the price of having a second implementation and it is
# the point of having one, so it is not waste -- but anybody who sets out to
# make the gate faster should know they are being asked to make the
# reference faster, and nothing else will matter.
#
# It already runs once a fixture rather than once a comparison: caching that
# took the sweep from nine minutes to two when it was written. What is left
# is four sequences a fixture, each independent of the others and each on one
# task.
#
# 2026-08-17, what the gate costs, stage by stage, as it now says itself:
#
#   repository checks     10.1 s
#   conformance         1425.1 s
#   fixtures              77.4 s
#   fuzzing                0.1 s
#
# Conformance is ninety-four per cent of it. That is worth knowing before
# anybody optimizes anything else: the fixture check's seventy-eight seconds
# looked like the thing that grew the gate, and it is five per cent of it.
#
# It also settles a question I had written down as work to do -- running the
# gate's halves in parallel. Overlapping the repository checks, the fixture
# check and both fuzzing campaigns with each other would save at most eighty-
# seven seconds of a twenty-five minute run, and they contend for the device
# besides. The only parallelism worth having is inside the conformance sweep,
# which is a different piece of work with a different argument.
#
# 2026-08-17, what the fixture check costs the gate. 78.3 s, the median of
# three on the host above: 76.30, 78.28 and 78.35, of which about 67 s is
# user time. It moves 22195 tensors, evaluating a tiny model twice for each.
#
# It cost 28 s when it was written, three days ago, and grew for two reasons
# worth separating: gemma3 is built with six blocks now rather than two,
# which is a third of the tensors it moves; and every fixture is also loaded
# by the independent implementation once, to compare what the file holds
# against what that implementation asks for.
#
# Not a measured figure in the sense the others are -- nothing publishes it
# and no fingerprint covers it -- but a gate that grew from thirty minutes
# is a thing somebody should be able to find the cause of, and 78 s of it is
# here.

# 2026-08-18, gpt2 on a published file. Six prompt tokens and twelve
# generated: 0.253 s wall -- 0.045 s evaluating the prompt and 0.210 s
# generating -- and 1.28 s of processor time, loading in 0.235 s, digest
# 6260472a109d3651.
#
# model: gpt2 Q8_0 as mradermacher published it -- gpt2.Q8_0.gguf,
# 177669376 bytes, 149 tensors, 163 million parameters, context 1024.
#
# Against TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0's 1.871 s for the same twelve tokens. Worth
# saying what that does and does not show: it is seven times faster and has
# about a seventh of the parameters, so what the figure measures is size. It
# is the first architecture here that does not rotate, and the rotation is
# too small a part of a token's work to see at this distance -- a figure
# that isolated it would have to hold the size fixed, which no pair of
# published models does.

# 2026-08-18, what a fixture has in common with a file somebody ships.
# `tests fixture-likeness --model PATH` folds layer indices together and
# compares tensor names both ways. Against the ten published files:
#
#   llama, phi2, phi3, falcon    every name on both sides
#   gpt2                         blk.*.ffn_norm.bias in the file, not in the
#                                fixture -- and not read by the engine
#   qwen2, qwen3, gemma,         output.weight written by the fixture and
#   gemma2, gemma3               carried by none of them: they tie the
#                                embedding, and no fixture here does
#
# The gpt2 line was a live defect, not a cosmetic difference. Every published
# gpt2 carries a shift beside its feed-forward normalization; the engine read
# every other layer-norm shift and not that one, and the feed-forward ran off
# a normalization that was neither centred nor shifted. Twenty-six thousand
# conformance sequences agreed with the reference throughout, because the
# reference did not read it either and the fixture did not write it. Three
# implementations sharing one wrong idea agree perfectly.
#
# The tied-embedding line is not a defect -- the engine falls back to the
# embedding when there is no output matrix, which is why those five models
# load and generate -- but it is a gap: no fixture here ties, so the fallback
# is exercised only by files a caller supplies.

# 2026-08-24, the weights stopped being copied.
#
# The file was mapped and the mapping served reads; the tensor section was
# then read into one arena, which is what "loading took 5.248 s" was on an
# eleven-gigabyte model, and what eleven gigabytes of resident memory was.
# The weights are the file's own pages now, and the arena is what a source
# that cannot be mapped still gets.
#
#   loading, TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0   0.065 s against 0.55 s
#   twelve tokens                  1.182 s wall, 0.262 evaluating and 0.920
#                                  generating, 8.33 s processor
#
# Loading is what moved and generation is not, which is the whole shape of
# the change: the same arithmetic reads the same bytes, and what stopped
# happening is a copy of them.
#
# What it does to a model too large to be comfortable, Qwen3-30B-A3B at
# 10.5 GiB, twelve tokens at a context of 512 and fifteen threads:
#
#   cold, cache dropped first      15.84 s wall, peak resident 4.6 GB
#   warm                           6.71 s wall, peak resident 4.6 GB
#
# Against 38.684 s of loading alone when the pages were dropped and 5.248 s
# when they were not, and against an arena of 11 252 639 744 bytes that was
# resident whether it was read or not. The resident figure is the mixture
# showing through: a token routes to eight experts of a hundred and
# twenty-eight, so most of the file is never touched and never faulted in.
# A dense model of the same size would touch all of it.
#
# Two things this cannot have at once, and the second is the one that had to
# be chosen against. A device opened to read the weights where they lie
# imports the host's own pointer, and this driver will not import a file's
# pages: asked for both, the run read 0 matrices where they lie and copied
# 633 233 408 bytes to the device. So a caller who names --device-memory 0
# gets the weights read into memory the device will take, and everyone else
# gets the file's pages. Written down because it is a fact about a driver
# rather than about this program, and the next driver may not have it.
#
# The rest of the figures below were taken again because quantization.adb,
# llama.adb and the tensor layer are sources behind all six groups. Nothing
# in them moved: the loading column did.

# 2026-08-24, one unit compiled twice, and four formats that wanted it.
#
# Q5_0 and Q5_1 keep the fifth bit of each element at a varying place in a
# thirty-two bit word, so the shift amount varies with the element; IQ4_NL and
# IQ4_XS index a table of sixteen levels, which is a gather. Neither
# vectorizes on baseline x86-64. The README has said so for months beside a
# note that building for a host with those instructions measured slower
# everywhere else, and that note was right and was never quantified. It is
# now, and then acted on.
#
# Three readings a build, alternating, the two binaries built before any of
# them so that no compile ran between measurements, each started below a load
# of 0.65. Whole build at -march=x86-64-v3 against the baseline, ns an
# element:
#
#   iq4nl 1.36 -> 0.75    q2_k  0.70 -> 0.81    q4_k 0.37 -> 0.52
#   q5_0  1.02 -> 0.64    q8_0  0.38 -> 0.47    q6_k 0.37 -> 0.55
#   q5_1  1.06 -> 0.68    q5_k  0.40 -> 0.53    bf16 0.32 -> 0.47
#   iq4xs 0.90 -> 0.68    q3_k  0.47 -> 0.63    q4_0 0.31 -> 0.48
#                         q4_1  0.43 -> 0.59    f32  0.25 -> 0.43
#                         f16   0.55 -> 0.59
#
# Four faster and eleven slower, by between seven and forty-two per cent. So
# not a build switch: the decoders are a generic instantiated twice, once with
# the project's switches and once with -march=x86-64-v3 -ffp-contract=off, and
# the four are sent to the second where the host says it has the instructions.
#
# Compiling only the decode wide beats compiling everything wide, and by more
# than the eleven-format loss explains. Accumulate_Dot decodes a span into a
# buffer and then multiplies it by each vector, and the second half is shared
# by every format: wide, it is slower, and it was dragging the four down with
# it. Split, on the same quiet machine:
#
#   iq4nl 1.36 -> 0.63, 2.16x     q5_1  1.06 -> 0.57, 1.86x
#   q5_0  1.02 -> 0.54, 1.89x     iq4xs 0.90 -> 0.50, 1.80x
#
# Eleven of the other formats sit within three per cent of where they were.
# The twelfth is q4_1, which reads 0.43 before and 0.50 after, eleven per cent
# slower, and nothing here explains it. The first guess was the call across
# the new unit boundary, which q4_1 pays per thirty-two elements where a
# k-quant pays it per two hundred and fifty-six; the block loop was moved
# inside the instance to test that and the number did not move. What is left
# is the thing the 2026-08-15 entry above describes -- these row products move
# with the layout of the unit they are compiled in, by up to a factor of two,
# in both directions, with no decoder changed -- and eleven per cent is well
# inside that band. It is written down rather than smoothed over.
#
# Everything else this touched, re-measured on the same quiet machine because
# quantization.adb is a source behind five groups of figures:
#
#   kernels, ns an element  f32 0.24, bf16 0.31, q4_0 0.31, q8_0 0.37,
#                           q6_k 0.38, q4_k 0.38, q5_k 0.40, q3_k 0.48,
#                           iq4xs 0.48, q4_1 0.49, q5_0 0.53, f16 0.54,
#                           q5_1 0.57, iq4nl 0.61, q2_k 0.71
#   twelve tokens           1.182 s wall, 0.262 evaluating and 0.920
#                           generating, 8.33 s processor, loading 0.065 s
#   at fourteen threads     1.258 s wall, 16.43 s processor
#   shares, pinned, q8_0    2660 Me/s at one, 13339 at seven, 14541 at eight;
#                           thirty-two a pass 4878, 24869, 27199
#   reference against cpu   8.986 s wall against 0.570 s, 5.397 and 3.602
#                           against 0.270 and 0.299, same digest; at the
#                           kernel 2.4x q8_0, 2.3x q4_k, 3.2x f32
#   device, short           1.191 s cpu against 0.950 s, of which 0.253 s
#                           and 0.164 s evaluating the prompt and 0.937 s
#                           and 0.785 s generating; 8.49 s processor against
#                           0.24 s
#   device, 110 tokens      4.841 s cpu against 2.043 s, 30.89 s processor
#                           against 1.18 s
#   drafting                1.174 s, 1.439 s and 4.801 s, 24 proposed and 9
#                           accepted
#
# The twelve-token figure is 1.155 s against yesterday's 1.315 on the same
# model, which is Q8_0 and takes none of the wide decoders. That is the layout
# again, in the direction that flatters, and it is quoted because it is what
# the tool says rather than because anything here earned it.

# 2026-08-23, the device table taken again, after the device path changed.
#
# The two-row table under "The device backend", all four cells adjacent, each
# waiting for the machine to fall below 1.50 before it started, medians of
# three:
#
#   cpu, six-token prompt, twelve generated   1.351 s wall, 0.271 s
#                                             evaluating and 1.087 s
#                                             generating, 8.71 s processor,
#                                             load 0.38 to 0.70
#   device, the same                          1.165 s wall, 0.200 s and
#                                             0.903 s, 0.30 s processor,
#                                             load 0.70 to 0.72
#   cpu, 110-token prompt, nothing generated  5.405 s evaluating, 32.46 s
#                                             processor, load 0.72 to 2.40
#   device, the same                          2.057 s evaluating, 1.17 s
#                                             processor, load 1.47 to 1.43
#
# Both backends print the same digests, 5abff916 and cbf29ce4.
#
# Why it was taken again: the sources behind these figures changed. The bound
# on a product's size stopped being a number chosen here and became the
# device's own answer, which is the change the checklist noticed and asked
# for these to be re-measured -- and it was right to, because the device
# column moved. It did not move because of the change. The same four cells
# were taken this morning on the code before it and again this afternoon on
# the code after it, and then the change was stashed and the afternoon pair
# taken a third time on the code before it: the long device run reads 1.212 s
# in the morning and 2.05 to 2.09 s in the afternoon whichever code is
# running, while the processor column repeats to within a per cent all day.
# What moved is the device's own state after a day of being measured, on a
# fifteen-watt part that shares its power with the processor beside it.
#
# The afternoon pair is published because both its columns were taken in one
# sitting. The morning device figures -- 1.321 s short and 1.212 s long -- are
# recorded here rather than dropped, because a reader taking a third reading
# should have two to put it beside rather than one.

# 2026-08-23, the whole Speed section taken again in one sitting. Every figure
# the README publishes there was retaken on a host that is its own again --
# the virtual machine that took two thirds of a processor through the last two
# sittings is gone -- so the section can be read against itself for the first
# time since it acquired that caveat.
#
# The library was at the release profile, which is what the tests crate pins
# it to; the harness around it was measured at both profiles and the kernel
# table came out the same to a hundredth of a nanosecond either way, which
# settles a question this file had not asked before: what the profile of the
# harness does to these numbers is nothing, because the work is in the
# library.
#
#   twelve tokens, processor    1.315 s wall, 0.282 evaluating and 1.033
#                               generating, 8.51 s processor, load 0.23-0.91
#   the same at fourteen threads 1.278 s wall, 16.40 s processor
#   twelve tokens, device       1.321 s wall, 0.192 and 0.947, 0.32 s processor
#   110 tokens, prompt only     5.376 s processor path, 32.00 s processor;
#                               1.212 s on the device for 0.26 s of processor
#   four tokens, reference      9.605 s wall against 0.648 s, same digest
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0 5.112 s, 24 proposed, 9 accepted
#   repack, q8_0 generation     1.014 s stored, 1.499 f32, 1.022 bf16
#   repack, q2_k generation     1.304 s stored, 1.363 f32, 0.955 bf16
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens     10.49 s at one, 5.05 s at 128
#   shares, pinned, q8_0        2501 Me/s at one, 13771 at eight
#   device residency            14.38, 3.07 and 1.65 tokens/s
#
# Three of these reversed a published reading rather than moving it. Fourteen
# threads now takes three per cent off the wall for ninety-three per cent more
# processor time, where the last sitting had it eleven per cent worse; the
# eighth share now pays where it used to cost six per cent; and repacking to
# f32 now costs time on both files where it used to pay on the two-bit one.
# All three have the same shape of cause: the kernels got fast enough that the
# run waits on memory, and every one of those three trades buys arithmetic
# with bytes.
#
# The device table stopped being a four-to-one win on the short run and became
# a dead heat, and nothing about the device moved. The processor side got
# three times faster since that table was taken. What the device still wins by
# is processor time -- 0.32 s against 8.51 -- and the prompt, where there is
# arithmetic enough to hide what a submission costs.
#
# The four row-product rows that swing with code layout swung back. q4_0 0.33,
# 0.59, 0.32; IQ4_NL 1.44, 0.59, 1.38; IQ4_XS 0.95, 0.48, 0.92; Q2_K 0.79,
# 1.01, 0.73. The 2026-08-15 entry above explains the first swing and no
# decoder has changed since, so it explains this one: these four are the
# gather-bound and shift-bound loops, and what they measure is the code and
# its layout together. Three runs at loads from 1.20 to 1.88, on two build
# profiles, agree to a hundredth of a nanosecond, so the reading is not the
# question.
#
# Two figures wanted a four-bit file of TinyLlama-1.1B that this machine did
# not have. One was fetched -- TheBloke's published Q4_K_M, 668 788 096 bytes,
# sha256 9fecc3b3cd76bba89d504f29b616eedf7da85b96540e490ca5824d3f7d2776a0,
# which replaces the locally requantized file the provenance page used to
# name -- and both were taken:
#
#   repack, q4_k_m generation   0.807 s stored, 1.107 f32, 0.835 bf16
#   four-bit against eight-bit  86.8 and 92.4 ms a token against 92.7 and
#                               89.3, alternating, two readings each
#
# Reading them needed something the old pair did not say. The four-bit file
# answers the short prompt in ten tokens and the eight-bit one in twelve, so
# a wall time compares two different amounts of work: 1.133 s against 1.400 s
# is the token count and not the format. The pair this replaces, 2.06 and
# 2.18 s against 2.06 and 2.26, was quoted as a wall time and had the same
# ten against twelve inside it with nothing saying so. The figures above are
# per token, and the repacking row carries its count.

# 2026-08-23, qwen3moe on a published file. Five prompt tokens and twelve
# generated: 6.665 s wall -- 1.715 s evaluating the prompt and 4.950 s
# generating -- and 41.68 s of processor time, digest 0721a909c5c304bb.
# Median of three at the default thread count, load 1.14 to 3.02.
#
# model: Qwen3-30B-A3B as published -- 11258610240 bytes, 579 tensors,
# 30 532 122 624 parameters of which about three billion run for a position,
# 128 experts of 768 with 8 of them chosen, context 40960, its tensors in
# Q2_K, Q3_K, Q4_K, Q6_K and F32.
#
# Beside TinyLlama-1.1B Q8_0, taken the same hour on the same machine by the
# same command: 1.306 s for the same twelve tokens. Five times the wall for
# about twenty-eight times the parameters, which is the mixture doing what a
# mixture is for -- what a token costs is the eight experts that run and not
# the hundred and twenty that do not. Against the 1.871 s this file publishes
# for that model the ratio is three and a half rather than five; that figure
# is an older engine, so the same-hour pair is the one to read.
#
# Loading is the part this figure does not carry, and for a file this size it
# is the part a caller notices. Eleven gigabytes cost 5.248 s to open with the
# file in the page cache and 38.684 s with its pages dropped from the cache
# first. The twelve tokens cost the same either way -- 6.458 s wall on the
# cold run, the same digest -- so what the cold read buys is entirely in the
# opening. A first run of the day is thirty-eight seconds of disk before it
# is anything else.
#
# And what `run` costs end to end, which is more than either: 15.6 s at the
# context the file declares and 12.1 s at 512, both warm and at fifteen
# threads. The difference is the session rather than the tokens -- 40960
# positions of key and value across 48 layers is the 8.4 GB `inspect` prints,
# allocated before the first token and not wanted by a caller who asked for
# twelve. The figure above is the tokens; this is what asking for them on
# this file costs.

# 2026-08-18, which architectures have been read from a file somebody else
# wrote. Every one of the ten the sweep crosses, validated with `tests
# external-model` -- it loads the file, generates, and checks that two runs
# agree and that the worker count does not change the answer:
#
#   llama   TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0        201 tensors, deterministic
#   gpt2    gpt2 Q8_0 (mradermacher)        149 tensors, deterministic
#   qwen2   Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct Q8_0        290 tensors, deterministic
#   qwen3   Qwen3-0.6B Q8_0                 310 tensors, deterministic
#   gemma   gemma-2b-it Q4_K_M (lmstudio)   164 tensors, deterministic
#   gemma2  gemma-2-2b-it Q4_K_M            288 tensors, deterministic
#   gemma3  gemma-3-1b-it Q4_K_M (unsloth)  340 tensors, deterministic
#   phi2    phi-2 Q4_K_M (TheBloke)         325 tensors, deterministic
#   phi3    Phi-3-mini-4k-instruct q4       195 tensors, deterministic
#   falcon  falcon-7b-instruct Q4_K_M       196 tensors, deterministic
#
# The eleventh architecture, qwen3moe, had no published file behind it for as
# long as this list said so: the smallest published one is a thirty-billion
# parameter mixture. It has one now.
#
# 2026-08-23, qwen3moe read from a published file.
#
#   qwen3moe  Qwen3-30B-A3B (Q2_K/Q3_K/Q4_K/Q6_K mixed, 10.5 GiB)
#             579 tensors, 30 532 122 624 parameters, deterministic,
#             thread-stable, twelve tokens generated on the processor
#
# `fixture-likeness` agrees with it name for name -- fifteen tensors a layer
# published, fifteen written by the fixture here, none unwritten and none
# invented -- which is the check that found gpt2's missing normalization
# shift on a file nobody here had written. It found nothing to report here:
# the mixture keys, the router and the three stacked expert tensors are
# spelled as this engine reads them.
#
# It is still not crossed by the sweep -- it is qwen3 with its metadata under
# another prefix, compared against the reference on its own -- but what
# stands behind it is no longer a fixture alone. That distinction is worth
# keeping visible for the architectures that still have only a fixture: a
# fixture and an engine can agree about a model nobody ships, which is what
# gpt2's output bias was, and no amount of agreement between two
# implementations reading the same fixture will say so.
#
# What it costs is below. What the file did find was in the template rather
# than in the arithmetic, and then in the device path.
#
# 2026-08-23, the mixture on a device, which had never been tried. It was
# refused, and the refusal named a capability the device had: an invented
# bound of 268 million elements stood where the device's own answer should
# have been, and every model whose output projection is wider than that --
# 151936 by 2048 is 311 million -- came to it. With the bound taken from the
# device instead, the mixture runs there:
#
#   weights read where they lie   12 tokens, prompt 85.495 s, generating
#                                 450.396 s, 0.03 tokens a second, 4094 of
#                                 4096 matrices read where they lie, 255 MB
#                                 on the device, context 512
#
# The same text the processor gives, which is the point of running it at all.
# It is a slow figure and it is a figure: what it says is that the device path
# survives 128 experts and a 30-billion-parameter file, and what it costs to
# read every weight across the bus for every token. Copied instead of read
# where they lie, the same run is refused before it starts -- 11251795968
# bytes wanted against a limit of 8472029184 -- which is the honest refusal
# the memory plan was always able to give and which the invented bound was
# standing in front of.
#
# 2026-08-23, and the tool-calling loop closed on it. The template rendering
# a tool conversation byte for byte against jinja2 says the prompt is right;
# it does not say the model does anything with it. Offered one tool -- a
# weather function taking a city -- and asked for the weather in Aarhus, the
# model answered with a call and nothing else: get_weather {"city":
# "Aarhus"}, reported by name on standard error as the caller needs it to be.
# Handed its own reply back with the tool's answer as a turn of its own, it
# read all three fields out of that answer and wrote them as a sentence: 17
# degrees, light rain, wind 22. Asked something that needs no tool with the
# same tool still offered, it answered directly and called nothing, which is
# the other half of the same check and the half a mis-rendered tools block
# would fail by calling for everything.
#
# Three runs at fifteen threads, thinking turned off, 78 s, 110 s and 73 s
# wall. That is the first time the calls a model asks for and the answers
# handed back to it have been through a published mixture rather than a
# fixture or a template comparison.
#
# Then two tools at once -- the weather function beside one that converts a
# currency -- which is where a single call stops proving much. Asked for both
# in one question, the model asked for both in one turn: get_weather {"city":
# "Aarhus"} and convert_currency {"amount": 100, "from": "EUR", "to": "DKK"},
# two calls in one reply, both read out of it and both reported by name.
# Handed that reply back with two answers as two turns, in the order the calls
# were made, it used both and kept them apart -- seventeen degrees and light
# rain for the first, 746.5 kroner for the second, in one sentence each.
# Asked something only one of the two tools can answer, it called that one and
# left the other alone, with the arguments read off the question rather than
# copied from the example: {"amount": 250, "from": "EUR", "to": "DKK"}.
#
# That is the branch of the template with the most in it -- the loop over a
# turn's calls, loop.first deciding where a newline goes, and the two answers
# folded into one user turn -- exercised by the model it was written for
# rather than by a conversation assembled here. 146 s, 202 s and 141 s wall.
# Nothing needed changing for any of it.
# The engine loaded it, generated from it and agreed with itself; `run`
# refused it, because the template a current mixture ships is written in a
# larger subset than the one a current dense model ships. Five things were
# missing and are there now, and the figures for them are in the changelog
# rather than here, because a template renders or it does not and there is
# nothing to time.
#
# One refusal along the way was not a defect. A gemma-2 file reported
# MR-GGUF-0022, a tensor running past the end of the file, which is exactly
# what a published gpt2 had legitimately found hours earlier. The download was
# 225 of 1708 megabytes. Whole, the same file loads and generates. The reader
# was right both times, and the second time it was right about the file rather
# than about the engine.

# covers:" line before a group says which published figures it stands
# for, and the check prints it when that group fails. A group whose figures
# are not named is a group nobody can answer: the reader learns that something
# changed and is left to guess what to measure again. If you add a figure to
# the README, add it to a covers line, and if it depends on a source no group
# lists, list it.
#
# On a mismatch the check prints the fingerprint it computed. Paste that in
# after re-measuring -- not before.
#
# These groups have twice been moved without re-measuring, which the rule
# above allows only with a reason. Both reasons are recorded here, because a
# reason that is not written down is a re-measure that was skipped.
#
# Supports_Batched in Backend.CPU.Describe: Describe had no caller in the
# program at all, so a field in it was not on any path these figures measure.
# Checked by searching the tree for callers, not assumed.
#
# The backend format check in Llama.Resolve: this one does run, once per
# resolved tensor. It runs during Prepare, and neither figure this group
# covers includes Prepare. The twelve-token figure says so outright -- load
# is quoted separately, "because the two are worth separating" -- and the
# batch-size table times prompt evaluation. The change is outside the window
# both figures measure, by their own definitions, which is a better answer
# than a number taken on a machine that at the time was on the powersave
# governor with other work on it.
#
# An A-B build against the previous commit was attempted and abandoned: a git
# worktree cannot resolve this crate's relative path pins.
#
# The capability checks in Llama: the format and alignment checks run during
# Prepare, outside the window as above. The Supports_Batched check runs inside
# it, once per Evaluate_Batch call, so that one was measured rather than
# argued. A-B-A on the same tree, the 131-token prompt with one token
# generated, three runs each: with the check 7.23 7.57 7.60, without it 7.47
# 7.56 7.89, with it again 7.35 7.26 7.76. The whole spread is wider than any
# difference between the states, which is the answer. The published table is
# not contradicted by these: they include load and one generated token.
#
# The Selecting_Backend progress event in Prepare: one Publish per model
# load, outside the window as above. The Prompt_Rendered event is in the
# command layer, which no group here fingerprints.
#
# The memory accounting at session open: a handful of additions, once per
# session, and session open is inside the window the twelve-token figure
# spans. Measured, not argued. A-B-A on the same tree, three runs each:
# with it 8.77 8.89 8.65, without one of the charges 8.73 9.04 9.06, with it
# again 8.71 9.08 8.68. The spread is wider than any difference between the
# states. These runs are on the 131-token prompt and include load, so they
# are not the published figure and are not offered as one.
#
# The session phases: an assignment at each phase boundary, inside the
# window. Measured: with them 8.66 8.35 9.07, with Enter doing nothing 9.09
# 9.38 8.88, with them again 8.84 8.97 9.12. Two evaluator assignments were
# also removed, which is why the middle state is not the faster one.
#
# The replaced-file check in Prepare: one stat of the file, once per model
# load, outside the window as above.
#
# Clearing the token history on reset, and recording a conversion: both
# happen once per reset and once per converted tensor at load, outside the
# window as above.
#
# The rotary pairing choice: two conditionals in the rotary inner loop,
# which is as inside the window as anything gets. Measured: with the choice
# 8.27 9.00 9.09, with the interleaved indices written straight in 9.34 9.28
# 9.04, with the choice again 8.94 9.03 8.86. The spread is wider than any
# difference between the states.
#
# Re-indenting three files to clear compiler style warnings moved all three
# groups at once. Not measured, and not argued either: the release build's
# instructions were compared before and after, and every difference is an
# immediate constant in exception-raising code -- the line number a raise
# reports, moved because blank lines were removed above it. Nothing that
# computes anything changed, which is a stronger statement than a timing and
# cheaper to make.
#
#   objdump -d --no-show-raw-insn obj/release/<unit>.o
#
# Routing every matrix product through the chosen backend: one case per
# product, inside the window. Measured, three runs each: through the choice
# 8.65 9.00 9.14, straight to the pool 8.73 8.97 8.79, through the choice
# again 8.78 8.82 8.84. There are about two thousand products in that run
# and the spread between repeats is wider than the difference between the
# states.
#
# The Capability accessor, read once per request when the batch size is
# clamped: with it 8.59 8.71 8.72, without it 8.65 8.77 8.97, with it again
# 8.76 8.78 8.84.
#
# Anything touching these files that is not outside the measured window gets
# a re-measure like that one, or a comparison like this one.

# Re-measure the two groups below with `tests benchmark`, which takes the
# median of three rounds itself. The pinned figures need
# `taskset -c 0-7 tests benchmark` on this machine -- one processor per core,
# read from the thread_siblings_list files; the README says so and why.
#
# Repacking moved both groups, and both were re-measured rather than argued
# about: the repacking code sits in llama.adb, which the engine group lists,
# and it changes the model's own preparation. Taken again on the machine and
# model above, with --repack off, which is what those figures describe:
# twelve tokens 0.32 s evaluating and 1.11 s generating against 0.26 and
# 1.02 published, 9.3 s of processor time at seven workers against 9.3 and
# 16.7 at fourteen against 17.2; the reference backend 0.32/1.15 against
# 5.67/11.43, which is 11.6x against the 12.5x published, and 2.44x, 2.45x
# and 3.14x at the kernel against 2.3x, 2.4x and 3.1x. Every one of those is
# inside the ten per cent this machine varies by, so the published figures
# still describe this code and were left alone.
#
# Moved again for the repacking rework -- a second target format, a skip for
# matrices already in it, and the decoding handed to tasks -- and this one was
# re-measured rather than reasoned about, because it is too large a change to
# read one's way through. With repacking off: twelve tokens 0.32 s evaluating
# and 1.02 s generating against 0.26 and 1.02 published, 9.3 s of processor
# time at seven workers and 17.1 at fourteen against 9.3 and 17.2; the
# reference backend 0.30/1.15 against 5.68/11.39, which is 11.8x against the
# 12.5x published, and 2.35x, 2.38x and 2.96x at the kernel against 2.3x,
# 2.4x and 3.1x. All inside the ten per cent this machine varies by.
#
# Moved twice more for repacking: a progress line, and the release of the
# file's bytes once the copy holds them. Both additions are inside
# `if Repack then`, and neither group is measured with repacking on -- the
# branch is one boolean test at load on the measured path and nothing else.
# The diffs are ten lines and two lines, which is cheaper to read than a
# measurement is to take.
#
# The earlier note stands for the first of them: The whole
# change to llama.adb is two lines: a Publish inside `if Repack then`, which
# neither group's figures use -- both are measured with repacking off, where
# that branch is one boolean test at load and not on any path either figure
# times. The diff is two added lines and nothing else, which is cheaper to
# read than a measurement is to take, and this is the kind of change the rule
# above allows a reason for instead.
#
# 2026-08-24, attention reads a position's values where they lie, and the
# rotation computes an angle once instead of once a head. Two changes,
# neither of which moves a bit: the blend held one component still and
# walked the whole cache for it, once for each component of a head, where a
# position's values are contiguous and one pass over them serves sixty-four
# components at a time; and Apply_Rotary sat its head loop outside its pair
# loop, so a power, a cosine and a sine were computed for every pair of
# every head when they depend on the pair and the position alone. Thirty-two
# heads is thirty-two times the transcendental calls the rotation needs.
#
# The same additions in the same order in both cases, so the run's digest is
# unchanged -- 448c2ed68ec342ee over sixty-four tokens and 5abff916f9d83ca6
# over twelve, as before -- and the conformance sweep moved no logit. That
# is what licenses reading the difference as the program:
#
#   110-token prompt, processor    4.558 s against 5.128 s, 24.1 t/s from 21.5
#   64 tokens generated, processor 5.230 s against 5.834 s, 12.2 t/s from 11.0
#   110-token prompt, device       1.982 s against 2.033 s
#   64 tokens generated, device    3.780 s against 3.940 s
#
# Everything below was re-measured in one sitting on this date, back to back,
# each run waiting for the machine to fall below 1.50 before it started. The
# twelve-token figure was taken three times within it and read 1.266, 1.198
# and 1.149 s; the middle is published and the spread is quoted in the README,
# because twelve tokens is a tenth of a second of arithmetic a token and the
# first run of a sitting pays for weight pages the later ones find touched.
#
# The kernel and share-scaling tables come from a `tests benchmark` run of
# their own, started at a load of 0.49: the first attempt ran at 2.75, inside
# the sitting that had just raised it, and a figure taken there is not one
# this repository publishes.
#
# The row-product table as taken, nanoseconds an element: f32 0.26, q4_0 0.31,
# bf16 0.32, q8_0 0.38, q4_k 0.39, q6_k 0.39, q5_k 0.41, q3_k 0.48, q4_1 0.50,
# iq4xs 0.51, q5_0 0.54, f16 0.56, q5_1 0.58, iq4nl 0.63, q2_k 0.72. Every one
# of them is a hair slower than the reading before, uniformly, on kernels
# nothing in this change touches -- which is what a machine looks like when it
# is a little slower today, and is why the table is re-taken whole rather than
# corrected where it looks wrong.
#
# The share-scaling figures are from a second run of the same tool under
# `taskset -c 0-7`, started at a load of 0.86, because the pinned case is the
# one the README argues from. Pinned, q8_0 with one vector a pass reads 2540
# Me/s serially and 13966 at eight shares against 12764 at seven; batched
# thirty-two a pass, 4655 serially and 26221 at eight against 24176 at seven.
# Unpinned, at 0.49: 13440 at eight against 12607 at seven with one vector,
# and 25598 against 22982 batched -- so unpinned eight is above seven on both
# now, where it fell below on the narrow one before. Four bits against eight,
# pinned: 14097 against 13966 at eight shares with one vector, 26625 against
# 26221 with thirty-two, 2525 against 2540 serially.
#
# The reference backend against the cpu one, serial, from the same quiet run:
# 2.4x for q8_0, 2.3x for q4_k, 3.1x for f32.

# 2026-08-24, activations quantized to a byte, and what re-measuring for it
# found. The engine gained Model_Runner.Quantization.Integers and a second
# entry into Tensors, both reached only when a run asks for --arith int8.
# The default path gained one null test per worker's share and nothing else,
# and every group below carries a source that changed, so every one of them
# was taken again in one sitting on this date.
#
# What came back is the sitting rather than the program. Loads of 1.3 to 2.9
# against 0.16 to 2.7 for the reading these figures were published from,
# because twenty-five measurements back to back are themselves the load, and
# every figure two to ten per cent slower with every digest identical:
#
#   twelve tokens             1.236 s against 1.198 published
#   110-token prompt          5.040 s against 4.558
#   64 tokens generated       5.630 s against 5.230
#   110-token prompt, device  1.975 s against 1.982
#   64 generated, device      4.703 s against 3.780
#   reference, four tokens    5.739 s and 3.826 against 5.541 and 3.704
#   drafting, the three runs  1.335, 1.512 and 4.951 s against 1.149, 1.453
#                             and 4.819
#
# The published cells stand and this reading is written down beside them
# rather than replacing them, for a reason the file should state plainly: a
# figure is only worth replacing when what moved is the program, and what
# moved here is the machine. The device generating row moved most, from
# 3.780 to 4.703 s, and it is the row this file has already called the
# noisiest -- three sittings have now read 13.3, 16.2 and 16.9 tokens a
# second on code that did not change it.
#
# The share-scaling table is the exception and is replaced, because its own
# run was the quietest any of them has had: started at a load of 0.10 under
# `taskset -c 0-7`, against 0.86 for the reading before. Pinned, q8_0 with
# one vector a pass reads 2633 Me/s serially and 14352 at eight shares
# against 13095 at seven; batched thirty-two a pass, 4801 serially and 26959
# at eight against 24583 at seven. Four bits against eight: 14312 against
# 14352 at eight shares with one vector, 26560 against 26959 with thirty-two,
# 2652 against 2633 serially.
#
# What the new arithmetic costs and buys is measured under its own option and
# published in "Quantized activations": 2.210 s against 4.940 on the prompt
# and 2.922 against 5.642 generating, at a conformance bound of 5.0E-2
# relative and 5.0E-1 absolute with nothing outside it over 28311 sequences.
# Those are not figures of these groups -- they are figures of a mode none of
# these groups runs -- and they are named here so that the next person to
# re-measure knows there are two arithmetics to re-measure in.

# 2026-08-24, the token budget re-taken, in the arithmetic a run uses.
#
# The budget measured the floating-point path, because `tests benchmark`
# never asked for the other one -- so since the default changed it had been
# describing a run nobody makes. It sets the arithmetic itself now, and
# reports the other one beside it. Pinned, at a load of 0.14, generating:
#
#   feed forward, gate up down       21.467 ms   69.1 %
#   attention projections, q k v o    6.521 ms   21.0 %
#   the vocabulary projection         1.985 ms    6.4 %
#   activation and gate               0.670 ms    2.2 %
#   softmax over the vocabulary       0.175 ms    0.6 %
#   normalization, twice a layer      0.156 ms    0.5 %
#   rotation                          0.101 ms    0.3 %
#   normalization, once at the end    0.004 ms    0.0 %
#   what a token costs of these      31.078 ms
#
# The same at --arith f32 is 73.752 ms; reading a prompt at thirty-two a pass
# is 12.066 ms a token.
#
# What it is for is deciding what to do next, and it decides two things.
#
# The products are 96.5 per cent of what it measures, so the two remaining
# ideas about the rest are dead: the rotation is 0.101 ms since its angles
# were tabulated and the transcendental kernels are 0.845 ms together, so a
# wider instruction set for the exponential -- tried and lost once already --
# is worth about two per cent of a token even if it wins outright.
#
# And a generated token really costs 44.2 ms against the 31.078 accounted
# for. The missing third is attention over the cache, which this tool cannot
# reach: the three blend kernels are inside Model_Runner.Llama with no entry
# point. At the first reading of this budget that gap was about a ninth of a
# token. The products have got 2.4 times faster since and attention has not,
# so it is now the largest single thing left on the processor -- and it is
# the one part of a forward pass that still runs entirely on the calling
# task while the worker pool sits idle.
#
# The row-product and share-scaling tables come from this same run. The row
# product reads f32 0.26, q4_0 0.30, bf16 0.32, q8_0 0.38, q4_k 0.39, q6_k
# 0.39, q5_k 0.41, q4_1 0.48, q3_k 0.49, iq4xs 0.51, q5_0 0.53, f16 0.56,
# q5_1 0.58, iq4nl 0.63 and q2_k 0.71 nanoseconds an element. Pinned, q8_0
# with one vector a pass reads 2542 Me/s serially and 14137 at eight shares
# against 12779 at seven; batched thirty-two a pass, 4648 serially and 25913
# at eight against 24253 at seven. Four bits against eight: 13987 against
# 14137 at eight shares with one vector, 26700 against 25913 with thirty-two,
# 2553 against 2542 serially.

# 2026-08-24, the integer product built twice, and a plan that turned out to
# be wrong about how much work it needed.
#
# The plan for this was a hand-written kernel reaching VPDPBUSD through a
# machine code insertion, with a third instruction-set level and a new host
# question to gate it. What it needed was one switch clause. The kernel is
# written around a sixteen-bit multiply-add, which is an instruction the
# wider sets have more and wider lanes of, and the compiler finds it without
# being told anything except which machine it is compiling for:
#
#                                 110-token prompt   64 generated
#   the baseline                       1.476 s          2.672 s
#   -march=x86-64-v3                   1.297 s          2.402 s
#   -march=x86-64-v4, 256-bit wide     1.308 s          2.366 s
#
# v4 is level with v3 and excludes far more hardware, so v3 is what is built,
# and the host question it needs -- avx2 and bmi2 -- is the one this program
# already asks for the decoders. No new level, no new host body, no assembly.
#
# One source and two compilations of it, as Decoders is, with contraction off
# on the wider one so that the two answer bit for bit and a test can say so.
#
# Everything taken again in one sitting at loads of 0.85 to 2.5:
#
#   twelve tokens              0.525 s wall, 0.079 evaluating and 0.442
#                              generating, 3.09 s processor
#   the same at --arith f32    1.298 s wall, 8.72 s processor, back to back
#   fourteen threads           0.519 s wall, 5.68 s processor
#   110-token prompt           1.319 s wall, 8.03 s processor
#   64 tokens generated        2.447 s wall, 14.86 s processor
#   1419-token prompt         29.286 s wall, 197.85 s processor
#   110-token prompt, device   1.679 s wall, 1.04 s processor
#   64 generated, device       3.510 s wall, 0.84 s processor
#   reference, four tokens     cpu 0.088 s and 0.158 s against 5.731 s and
#                              3.779 s
#   drafting, the three runs   0.532 s, 1.668 s and 3.639 s, 24 proposed and
#                              7 accepted
#   batch sizes 1 to 128       4.062, 2.337, 1.742, 1.510, 1.382, 1.392,
#                              1.434 and 1.300 s on the processor
#   llama.cpp on the same file 377.69 and 39.92 tokens a second on the
#                              processor, 1666.53 and 56.40 on the device,
#                              762.61 and 39.97 with -ngl 0
#
# The device table, cell for cell: six-token prompt with twelve generated,
# cpu 0.532 s against device 1.025 s; evaluating the prompt 0.097 s against
# 0.189 s; generating 0.453 s against 0.769 s; processor time 3.11 s against
# 0.27 s; 110-token prompt cpu 1.384 s against device 1.679 s, processor time
# 8.15 s against 1.04 s.
#
# The row-product and share-scaling tables come from the pinned run of the
# same sitting, started at a load of 0.72. The row product reads f32 0.27,
# q4_0 0.31, bf16 0.32, q8_0 0.38, q4_k 0.39, q6_k 0.39, q5_k 0.41, q3_k
# 0.49, q4_1 0.50, iq4xs 0.51, q5_0 0.55, f16 0.57, q5_1 0.60, iq4nl 0.64 and
# q2_k 0.73 nanoseconds an element -- all a shade slower than the sitting
# before, uniformly, on kernels this change does not touch. Pinned, q8_0 with
# one vector a pass reads 2538 Me/s serially and 13761 at eight shares
# against 12798 at seven; batched thirty-two a pass, 4632 serially and 25616
# at eight against 24247 at seven. Four bits against eight: 13732 against
# 13761 at eight shares with one vector, 26613 against 25616 with thirty-two,
# 2535 against 2538 serially.
#
# The device's 110-token prompt is 1.679 s here, which is the open question
# the entry above describes and not a new one.

# 2026-08-25, the tile a product takes is chosen from the batch rather than
# fixed at four, so every group naming the kernel or the tensor package moved
# and all seven were taken again in one sitting, load under 1.20 before each
# run.
#
#   twelve tokens, seven workers    0.550 s wall, 3.10 s processor
#   the same at fourteen threads    0.527 s wall, 5.65 s processor
#   four tokens, reference          9.708 s wall against 0.545 s
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.545 s   device 0.556 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.092 s   device 0.066 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.465 s   device 0.489 s
#     processor time                cpu 3.10 s    device 0.03 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.284 s   device 0.589 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.64 s    device 0.12 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.528 s   device 2.744 s
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens         4.264 s at one, 1.208 s at 128
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0     3.582 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#   row dot, q8_0                   2511 Me/s, 0.40 ns an element
#   device ratios                   q8_0 0.56 one vector, 0.14 at eight,
#                                   0.043 at thirty-two; load 1.14 to 2.61
#   llama.cpp, processor            389.6 t/s prompt, 40.2 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1671.5 t/s prompt, 57.7 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             782.3 t/s
#
# The tile is eight where a pass carries more than one vector and four where
# it carries one, and what that is worth is three per cent of the prompt with
# generation level -- three rounds alternated, the prompt faster in every one.
#
# What the change is really about is that four had gone stale without
# anything noticing. It was measured -- 2.120 s at two, 1.948 at four, 2.122
# at eight -- when a prompt was read thirty-two vectors at a time. A tile
# reads the activation once and the activation is re-read once per tile, so
# what a larger tile saves grows with the batch, and when the default became
# a hundred and twenty-eight the answer moved: 1.379 at four, 1.351 at six,
# 1.281 at eight. Nothing asked the question again, because a constant
# carries no fingerprint. That is the same failure as a figure resting on a
# source no group names, one level down.
#
# 2026-08-25, later. Two changes, and a hole in this file that is worth more
# than either of them.
#
# The hole: no group named the integer kernel. Not one of the seven lists
# above held model_runner-quantization-integers.adb or any of its children,
# and that is the arithmetic every processor figure in this file is measured
# through -- it has been the default since --arith int8 became it. The kernel
# could be rewritten, and was, with nothing here asking for a re-measure.
# All seven groups name its six files now.
#
# That is the third time in two days: the default batch lived in a file no
# group named, the tile a product takes was a constant no fingerprint could
# cover, and now the kernel itself. The pattern is that a fingerprint follows
# what a figure is *computed by* and keeps missing what it is *decided by*,
# and the three of them together are the argument for reading these lists
# against the code rather than trusting that they grew right.
#
#   The sitting, load under 1.20 before every run:
#
#   twelve tokens, seven workers    0.565 s wall, 3.10 s processor
#   the same at fourteen threads    0.522 s wall, 5.52 s processor
#   four tokens, reference          9.733 s wall against 0.536 s
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.536 s   device 0.600 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.082 s   device 0.073 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.454 s   device 0.527 s
#     processor time                cpu 3.10 s    device 0.05 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.292 s   device 0.534 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.16 s    device 0.11 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.519 s   device 3.039 s
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens         4.303 s at one, 1.279 s at 128
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0     3.769 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#   row dot, q8_0                   2501 Me/s, 0.40 ns an element
#   device ratios                   q8_0 0.55 one vector, 0.14 at eight,
#                                   0.042 at thirty-two; load 1.10 to 1.72
#   llama.cpp, processor            384.8 t/s prompt, 40.1 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1672.3 t/s prompt, 57.5 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             773.9 t/s
#
#   The device generating row reads 3.039 s here against 2.744 in the sitting
#   before, which is eleven per cent where the alternated rounds that chose
#   the wider group put the cost at two. That row has read 13.3 to 24.2
#   tokens a second across a dozen sittings on code that did not change it,
#   and this file says so beside the table; the figure is what this sitting
#   measured and the three rounds are what the choice rests on.
#
# The two changes, both measured by alternating three rounds:
#
#   The block product goes through a machine code insertion. The eight
#   partial sums a sixteen-bit multiply-add leaves were reduced to a scalar
#   at every block -- about seven instructions of shuffling for three of
#   arithmetic -- and an ablation put the whole multiply-add step at 39 per
#   cent of a prompt at about a fifth of the rate this part can issue. The
#   insertion folds them to four, keeps those across a row and reduces once.
#   The prompt reads 1.280 s against 1.323, better in each of three rounds;
#   generating is level and the digest does not move. There are two
#   insertions, one per instruction set, arranged so a sum holds the same
#   four of a block's sixteen pairs either way: the test that both
#   compilations answer the same bits is what forced that, and it caught the
#   first version, which did not.
#
#   A device workgroup is 256 invocations rather than 64. Nothing had ever
#   varied it -- it was the wave width of the part this was written against.
#   The device prompt reads 0.547 s against 0.579, better in each of three
#   rounds; generating reads about two per cent slower, which is inside that
#   row's spread.
#
# Two more were measured and dropped. Rows a tile beyond eight buy nothing:
# 1.305 s at eight, 1.273 at twelve, 1.309 at sixteen, 1.316 at twenty-four,
# with the spread inside a setting as wide as the gaps between them -- so the
# activation traffic a larger tile saves has stopped being what the prompt
# waits on. And naming the next block of each row with a prefetch makes
# generating worse in each of three rounds, 2.591 s against 2.533, with the
# prompt a wash: the streams are sequential and the part already had them.
#
# 2026-08-25, the byte dot product. A third compilation of the integer
# kernel, for x86-64-v4, reaching VPDPBUSD through a machine code insertion.
# All seven groups name that kernel, so all seven were taken again.
#
#   twelve tokens, seven workers    0.514 s wall, 3.01 s processor
#   the same at fourteen threads    0.509 s wall, 5.35 s processor
#   four tokens, reference          9.644 s wall against 0.513 s
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.513 s   device 0.437 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.074 s   device 0.051 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.428 s   device 0.385 s
#     processor time                cpu 3.04 s    device 0.03 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.196 s   device 0.583 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.34 s    device 0.12 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.353 s   device 2.082 s
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens         4.183 s at one, 1.231 s at 128
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0     3.548 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#   row dot, q8_0                   0.39 ns an element
#   device ratios                   q8_0 0.41 one vector, 0.09 at eight,
#                                   0.038 at thirty-two; load 1.09 to 2.15
#   llama.cpp, processor            381.5 t/s prompt, 40.1 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1681.3 t/s prompt, 57.8 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             776.5 t/s
#
# What the instruction bought, alternated three rounds against the sixteen-bit
# path: the prompt 1.222 s against 1.271 and generating 2.354 against 2.496,
# better in every round of both.
#
# And what nearly buried it. VPDPBUSD is unsigned against signed, so the
# weight byte is biased by 128 and the bias taken back out with the
# activation block's own sum -- the Totals table, which this kernel is handed
# and does not otherwise read. Accumulated as a scalar read-modify-write in
# the innermost loop, that correction cost fifteen per cent of a prompt,
# which is more than the instruction saved and would have been recorded here
# as another negative. Built once a block as a vector holding the whole
# correction in its first lane, and added inside the insertion as one integer
# add, it is the figures above. Same instruction, same arithmetic, different
# place to put four bytes.
#
# A single reading taken straight after that fix said 1.289 s -- still losing.
# Three alternated rounds said 1.222. Every single reading this session that
# disagreed with a three-round median has been the wrong one.
#
# This is also the first change in the program whose answer depends on the
# host's instruction set. The two sixteen-bit compilations still agree bit for
# bit; the byte one groups a block's products differently into its lanes and
# is held to the sweep's five per cent instead, which is written up under
# "And a third, which does not answer the same bits".
#
# 2026-08-25, the block loop moved inside the insertion for a single
# vector. All seven groups name the kernel and were taken again.
#
#   twelve tokens, seven workers    0.470 s wall, 2.68 s processor
#   the same at fourteen threads    0.459 s wall, 4.74 s processor
#   four tokens, reference          9.743 s wall against 0.479 s
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.479 s   device 0.449 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.081 s   device 0.062 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.396 s   device 0.387 s
#     processor time                cpu 2.69 s    device 0.03 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.225 s   device 0.586 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.33 s    device 0.12 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.148 s   device 2.058 s
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens         3.728 s at one, 1.292 s at 128
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0     3.549 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#   row dot, q8_0                   0.40 ns an element
#   device ratios                   q8_0 0.40 one vector, 0.09 at eight,
#                                   0.037 at thirty-two; load 1.13 to 3.13
#   llama.cpp, processor            387.1 t/s prompt, 40.2 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1678.8 t/s prompt, 57.6 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             779.9 t/s
#
# What it bought, alternated three rounds: sixty-four generated in 2.187 s
# against 2.400, better in every round and steady to a hundredth. The prompt
# does not enter the path at all -- the condition is one vector -- and its
# readings across the same six runs were 1.192 to 1.300 s. That accidental
# control is the most useful number in this entry: it puts the noise floor on
# this machine at about five per cent, which is the size of several changes
# measured today, and is why single readings keep being wrong here.
#
# The shape, and why it is only for a generated token. A block at a time
# loads the accumulator, multiplies, and puts it back; a whole row inside one
# insertion keeps it in a register from the first block to the last. That is
# the load and the store gone and a fused multiply-add in place of a separate
# multiply and add -- about eleven instructions a block down to seven.
#
# It works here because a token multiplies one vector, so the accumulators
# are one a row: four registers. A prompt multiplies a hundred and
# twenty-eight, which is a thousand accumulator sets against sixteen
# registers, and holding them means re-reading the weights once per vector
# tile -- sixteen passes over a gigabyte -- unless the weights are packed
# into cache-sized panels first. That packing is the remaining work and is
# not here.
#
# 2026-08-25, two things measured and neither kept. Nothing here moved;
# this is recorded so the next reader does not build them again.
#
# A register-blocked tile for the prompt, four rows by four vectors.
#
# The shape a blocked matrix product has: sixteen accumulators living in
# registers from a row's first block to its last, the weights packed by block
# so a block's four rows are contiguous, the scale applied by an embedded
# broadcast, and the whole thing in the deepest compilation because sixteen
# accumulators want more than sixteen registers. It is written, it is
# correct -- the sweep passed with nothing outside tolerance -- and across
# three attempts it does not beat the kernel it replaces:
#
#   as first written          1.234 s against 1.254, spread 1.192 to 1.357
#   packing hoisted out of
#     the vector-group loop   1.243 s against 1.291, better in three rounds
#   scales gathered before
#     the innermost loop      1.299 s against 1.249, worse in two of three
#
# Against a noise floor of about five per cent, none of those is separated
# from nothing. Two real defects were found and fixed on the way -- the
# weights were being packed once per group of four vectors rather than once
# per chunk, thirty-two times over; and the innermost loop computed an index
# with a division in it -- and fixing them moved the figure inside the noise
# and no further.
#
# Why it cannot pay, which is the part worth keeping. Tiling amortises loads
# and nothing else. Per row, per vector, per block the work is a zero, a
# multiply-add, a widening and a fused multiply-add -- four vector
# instructions -- plus a scale multiply and a bias multiply-add in scalar.
# None of those six is shared between the tile's elements, because every one
# of them carries its own pair of scales. A tile of sixteen shares four
# weight loads and four activation loads across sixteen elements, and that is
# the whole of what it buys: about 5.3 multiply-accumulates an instruction
# against 4.6. Fifteen per cent of ceiling, and the packing and the gathering
# spend it.
#
# Which says something about the four-times gap to the other runtime that no
# amount of this will close. Its inner loop pays the same six operations per
# element -- the same instruction mix, the same scale multiply. At 387 tokens
# a second it is sustaining about 2.5 instructions a cycle a core; this
# program at 91 is sustaining about 0.7. The prompt gap is not a
# multiply-accumulates-per-instruction problem and cannot be fixed by
# arranging the arithmetic better. It is stalls, and finding them wants a
# profiler: perf_event_paranoid is 4 on this host, so nothing here has ever
# seen a cycle counter.
#
# And the device shader's decode, measured rather than repacked.
#
# Built with the eight-bit branch's decode replaced by a constant -- wrong
# answers, right shape -- the device prompt reads 0.415, 0.427 and 0.452 s
# against 0.573 with the real one. So unpacking the weights is about a
# quarter of what the shader spends, and a repack that removed all of it
# would take the device prompt from 191 tokens a second to about 258.
#
# That is worth having and it is not what it looks like. The bytes are
# already bytes; what costs is that the buffer is bound as words and every
# weight is extracted with a shift and a mask. The way to it is
# GL_EXT_shader_8bit_storage and a binding of the right type, not a repack of
# the model -- a repack that kept the byte count could not remove the
# extraction, and one that did not keep it would cost four times the device
# memory.
#
# 2026-08-25, the sitting that goes with the counter entry below. All seven
# groups name the kernel, so all seven were taken again.
#
#   twelve tokens, seven workers    0.480 s wall, 2.71 s processor
#   the same at fourteen threads    0.445 s wall, 4.75 s processor
#   four tokens, reference          9.729 s wall against 0.481 s
#   six-token run                   cpu 0.481 s   device 0.428 s
#     evaluating the prompt         cpu 0.086 s   device 0.051 s
#     generating                    cpu 0.397 s   device 0.378 s
#     processor time                cpu 2.70 s    device 0.04 s
#   110-token prompt                cpu 1.207 s   device 0.567 s
#     processor time                cpu 7.18 s    device 0.12 s
#   sixty-four generated            cpu 2.203 s   device 2.280 s
#   batch sweep, 110 tokens         3.863 s at one, 1.195 s at 128
#   drafted, q2_k drafting q8_0     3.674 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#   row dot, q8_0                   0.40 ns an element
#   device ratios                   q8_0 0.36 one vector, 0.08 at eight,
#                                   0.036 at thirty-two; load 1.08 to 1.28
#   llama.cpp, processor            386.7 t/s prompt, 40.4 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp, device              1686.7 t/s prompt, 57.4 t/s generating
#   llama.cpp at -ngl 0             778.5 t/s
#
# The device generating row reads 2.280 s where the sitting before it read
# 2.001, on a shader that did not change between them. That row has read 13.3
# to 32.0 tokens a second across a dozen sittings and this file says so beside
# the table; the figure is what this sitting measured.
#
# 2026-08-25, a counter, at last. perf_event_paranoid was 4 on this machine
# for every figure in this file until now; lowered to 1, the 110-token
# prompt on the processor reads 96.3 thousand million instructions in 28.0
# thousand million cycles -- 3.43 a cycle -- with a 1.3 per cent cache miss
# rate and 1.9 per cent of cycles stalled at the front end.
#
# That kills what this file said two entries above. The prompt is not losing
# to stalls and never was: 3.43 a cycle is near what the part retires, and
# above the 2.5 that was attributed to the other runtime. The 0.7 written
# here earlier was a measured time divided by a guessed instruction count,
# and the guess was out by a factor of four.
#
# What it costs is instructions: ninety-six thousand million for something
# near a hundred and ten thousand million multiply-accumulates, twenty-odd
# instructions for each one that multiplies. perf annotate puts about twenty
# instructions in the hot loop with exactly one vpdpbusd among them.
#
# One change came out of it and is kept. The byte instruction's memory
# operand needs no alignment, so the activations are read where the quantizer
# left them instead of being copied into a block of their own first -- a copy
# of thirty-two bytes shared between eight rows, which is about four
# instructions for every multiply-add. 96.3 thousand million instructions to
# 93.2, and about five per cent of a prompt: 1.191 s against 1.251, better in
# two rounds of three, and the instruction count is what settles it because
# it does not vary between runs.
#
# Two did not, and both are worth the space:
#
#   Four rows to one insertion, so that one address computation and one
#   scale lookup serve four multiply-adds. It executes MORE instructions than
#   the loop it replaces -- 98.7 thousand million against 96.3 -- and reads
#   1.328 s against 1.269. The hand count that predicted otherwise was the
#   third such count this week to be wrong, and the counter was available
#   this time.
#
#   It also had a defect the sweep did not catch and the kernel's own test
#   did: the lane type carried Alignment => 32 on sixteen bytes of data, so
#   every entry is padded to thirty-two and a hand-written stride of sixteen
#   reads the wrong halves. An aspect that pads is a stride nobody declared.
#
# What is left of the prompt gap is a question rather than a plan: twenty
# instructions a multiply, and no attempt so far has moved that number by
# more than a few per cent. It wants somebody to read the annotated loop
# properly rather than another rewrite measured after the fact.
#
# covers: the row-product table under "Kernels"
# 2026-08-11, IQ4_NL and IQ4_XS. Re-measured rather than argued about, and
# they could be, because these two groups need no model: `tests benchmark`
# and `taskset -c 0-7 tests benchmark` are the whole of it.
#
# The row-product table is republished from one unpinned sitting, all fifteen
# formats together, and it gained the two new rows -- 1.37 and 0.92 ns an
# element, which makes them the slowest in the table. The existing formats
# read a few per cent slower than the figures they replace, which is what
# this README already says happens between sittings; nothing about them
# changed and the table is now internally consistent instead of spanning two
# sittings.
#
# The share figures are republished from one pinned sitting, taken with
# nothing else running. Eight shares on q8_0 with one vector reads 12420
# against the 13134 published before, and seven 12007 against 12532; the
# claim those numbers are there to support -- that eight no longer falls
# below seven -- still holds, and holds in this sitting rather than in the
# last one.
#
# One figure moved enough to say so rather than swap quietly: the four-bit
# format at thirty-two vectors and eight shares came out 17820 against the
# 23450 published, while its own seven-share figure was 20023. It falls off
# above four shares in every run taken here. The README now says that instead
# of claiming the two formats are level everywhere.
# 2026-08-11, the half-precision cache. tensors.adb gained an allocator and a
# deallocator for a half-precision buffer and numerics.ads a type; no loop
# either of these groups times was touched, and neither group's figures are
# taken through a session at all -- the kernel benchmarks build their own
# tensors and multiply them. Not re-measured: these two were taken again
# three commits ago on this machine, and taking them again for a type
# declaration would say what that run already says.
# 2026-08-15, phi3. Four groups moved with llama.adb and are not
# re-measured: everything phi3 adds runs while a model loads. The layer
# resolution asks once per layer which architecture it is, and Resolve_Part
# makes a view; nothing was added to an evaluation loop, which the diff
# shows in one reading.
#
# Measured twice all the same, and the pair is worth recording for what it
# says about this machine rather than about phi3: 2.089 s and 11.95 s of
# processor time at a load rising to 2.41, and 1.912 s and 10.97 s at a load
# that stayed at 1.5. The published figure is 1.88 s. A fifteen per cent
# spread between two runs an hour apart is what a busy machine does, and it
# is why the first of the two was not written down as a regression.

# 2026-08-15, gemma3. Four groups moved with llama.adb and are not
# re-measured. The twelve-token figure reads 1.809 s and 10.29 s of
# processor time against 1.832 s and 10.51 s taken the same way an hour
# earlier, which is inside what consecutive runs of this tool differ by.
#
# What these groups see of gemma3 is a function call per rotation that
# returns the model's base unless the model states a second one -- and one
# comparison per layer to decide it. The lesson from gemma2 was applied
# before measuring rather than after: the work that only one architecture
# needs is kept out of the loops the others run.

# 2026-08-15, gemma2. Four groups moved with llama.adb and are not
# re-measured; the twelve-token figure is the evidence, taken twice.
#
# The first version of this change applied the attention bound inside the
# loop that forms a score, which cost every architecture a test per score:
# twelve tokens went from 1.83 s to 2.07 s and the processor time from
# 10.5 s to 11.8 s, for a feature one architecture of six uses. The bound
# runs in a loop of its own now, entered only when there is one, and the
# figure reads 1.832 s and 10.51 s against the 1.828 s and 10.53 s measured
# before gemma2 existed.
#
# That is the whole of what these groups see of it: a loop that does not
# run, a null check before a normalization that does not happen, and a test
# on a zero cap when the logits come out.

# 2026-08-15, gemma, and a lesson about where a figure comes from.
#
# The row-product table moved by up to a factor of two in both directions --
# q4_0 from 0.33 to 0.59 ns an element, IQ4_NL from 1.44 to 0.59, IQ4_XS from
# 0.95 to 0.48, Q2_K from 0.79 to 1.01 -- and nothing in any decoder changed.
# What changed is that kernels.adb gained a procedure: the Gaussian gate that
# gemma wants, which no row product calls.
#
# Two runs an hour apart agree on the new numbers, so it is not the machine.
# Adding a subprogram to a hot compilation unit moves what the compiler does
# with the rest of it, and this unit is where every decoder lives. The
# published table is the new one, and the old one is not evidence of a
# regression or an improvement in anything: it is the same code compiled
# beside different code.
#
# The reference, drafting and scaling groups are not re-measured. The
# twelve-token figure is the evidence for all three: it runs the same
# engine paths through the same worker pool, it moved by less than these
# runs move between themselves, and the code they cover changed in exactly
# the two ways it did -- a branch in RMS_Norm and a test on the embedding
# scale, both taken on every architecture. The scaling group's figures are
# pinned row products and would want a pinned run to say more than that,
# which the machine has not offered again today.
#
# The device ratios come from the same run and are republished with it. The
# twelve-token figure was retaken too -- 1.828 s against 1.88 s at a load of
# 1.42 to 1.55 -- which is what says the engine paths themselves did not
# move: the branch gemma adds to RMS_Norm and the test on the embedding
# scale are both there for every architecture now.

# 2026-08-14, waiting for a quiet machine moved into the tools. Three groups
# moved with benchmarks.adb. Not re-measured: what was added is a wait before
# any timing starts and a procedure that prints while it waits. Neither runs
# once measuring has begun -- Wait_For_Quiet returns before the first round,
# and with no --wait it is not called at all, which is how every figure in
# this file was taken.

# 2026-08-14, the pinned figures retaken, which was the last group with no
# load recorded. Eight shares reads 10122 Me/s with one vector against the
# published 12420, and seven reads 10713 against 12007 -- so the peak at
# seven that the earlier run had removed is back, at six per cent rather
# than the quarter the change was made for. Batched there is no peak: 21234
# at eight against 21035 at seven.
#
# The four-bit against eight-bit numbers moved too, and swapped sides: the
# four-bit format now leads serially and batched and trails at eight shares
# with one vector, where last time it was the reverse. Two runs disagreeing
# about which is ahead at one shape is what level looks like, and the README
# says that rather than picking the run that reads better.

# 2026-08-14, the tokenizer figures taken by the tool. 0.0098 s for sixty
# thousand ordinary characters and 0.0127 s for sixty thousand brackets, at
# a load of 1.12 -- against the 0.039 and 0.045 timed in the shell, which
# were a whole `model_runner run` rather than the encode. The row-product
# table and the thirty device ratios come from the same run and are
# republished with them.

# 2026-08-14, the worker-count pair retaken -- and it does not say what the
# paragraph built on it said. Fourteen threads reads 1.536 s of wall and
# 16.12 s of processor time against seven at 1.884 s and 10.42 s, both at a
# load of about 1.25. That is eighteen per cent off the wall for fifty-five
# per cent more processor time, where the published text said six per cent
# for seventy.
#
# The old numbers came from the shell's timer, on runs whose load nobody
# recorded, and included the model load that these exclude. The conclusion
# stands -- one worker per core -- but it stands on energy alone now rather
# than on the wall being nearly unchanged, and the README says so. This is
# the third figure this week that moved once it was taken again with its
# conditions written down, and the second whose stated reasoning had to be
# rewritten rather than just its digits.

# 2026-08-14, the tokenizer timed by a tool rather than by the shell. Three
# groups moved because benchmarks.adb gained a section. Not re-measured, and
# the reason is order: the new section runs after the row products, the
# device ratios and the share scaling, so it cannot have affected a figure
# taken before it started. Read Run to check that rather than take it on
# trust -- it is one call list, and the tokenizer lines sit between the
# vector kernels and the metadata parse.

# 2026-08-14, processor time from this repository's own tool. `tests speed`
# reports it beside the wall time, taken around the same region, which
# removes the one figure in this file that came from the shell. The reason
# it did not before -- that totalling processor time across worker tasks
# needs a host call bound per platform -- was wrong: it is the same file the
# load comes from.
#
# The twelve-token figure was retaken with it: 1.884 s and 10.39 s of
# processor time at a load of 1.28 to 1.82. Reference, device and drafting
# were not, and the reason is what that figure shows: the same measurement
# read 1.869 s before this change and 1.884 s after, which is inside the
# spread two consecutive runs of this tool have. What was added is two reads
# of one small file per repeat, one just inside each end of the timed
# region -- microseconds against seconds, and inside rather than outside
# because a processor time taken around a different region than the wall
# would answer about different work.
#
# The worker-count pair was not retaken either.
# Each half of that comparison needs its own quiet window and the machine
