#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b.
#
# 2026-08-31, the generated token carried too, and the host's copy of its
# cache read back once a token rather than once a layer.
#
#   Three commits ago the batched evaluator stopped bringing a layer's
#   answer home between layers and stopped mirroring its keys and values a
#   layer at a time. The single-position evaluator -- which is what
#   generating uses, and which is where the host's share of a token's time
#   is -- never got either. It is the same three things there:
#
#     Carry_In and Carry_Out, so the first layer of a token reads what the
#     host has, the last writes what it read, and the ones between hand the
#     activation on where the device left it.
#
#     Mirror off while chaining, so a layer does not wait on its own fence
#     to copy one position of keys and values back.
#
#     A pass after the loop that reads those positions out of the device's
#     own cache for every layer that deferred them. One position apiece,
#     which is what a token writes, against twenty-two waits.
#
#   Whole_Layer_Fits is asked of the next layer as well as of this one: a
#   layer that falls back to the host reads the host's copy of the
#   activation, and the host's copy is exactly what carrying does not
#   write.
#
#   Alternated, sixty-four generated on the device, medians of three:
#     chained    1.269, 1.273, 1.286 s
#     waiting    1.351, 1.336, 1.369 s
#   Ahead in all three rounds, about five per cent. An earlier sitting on
#   the same pair read 1.249 and 1.272 against 1.316 and 1.331, ahead in
#   five of five. Eight pairs, eight wins.
#
#   What said to look here: a generated token dispatches 18.29 ms of work

# load:  0.88 to 3.44 across the sitting, each pair adjacent
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-08-31, llama.cpp's matrix tile, built here and slower, with its two
# halves separated; and the one part of it that was kept.
#
#   Where the device's prompt stands, profiled on both sides at 1419
#   tokens -- `speed --budget` here, GGML_VK_PERF_LOGGER there:
#
#     matrix products   0.828 s (79.1 %)   against 0.581 s (77 %)  1.42x
#     attention         0.160 s (15.3 %)   against 0.078 s (10 %)  2.06x
#     norm/rope/join    0.024 s            against 0.093 s         ahead
#
#   Eighty-four per cent of the whole gap is the first row. The kernel runs
#   that work at 3.4 teraflops against llama.cpp's 4.9.
#
#   Their tile was read out of ggml-vulkan.cpp rather than derived. For an
#   AMD device with VK_KHR_cooperative_matrix on the open driver the large
#   quantized warptile is {256, 128, 128, 32}: BM 128, BN 128, BK 32, two
#   hundred and fifty-six invocations as four subgroups, with both operands
#   staged in shared memory at a padded stride and every subgroup loading
#   its quarter from there. Ours is 32 by 128 by 32 on one subgroup, with
#   the weights staged and the batch loaded where it lies.
#
#   Built. It answers 1a26d24d33b8957b, which is the published digest, and
#   it is thirty-one per cent slower. So the two halves were separated,
#   because a change that loses says nothing about which of its parts lost.
#
#   Alternated round by round, medians of three, the 1419-token device
#   prompt, three rounds each:
#
#     32 rows, one subgroup, batch in place   1.026, 1.029, 0.990 s
#     the same, batch staged                  1.195, 1.195, 1.196 s
#     128 rows, four subgroups, in place      1.210, 1.170, 1.152 s
#     128 rows, four subgroups, staged        1.375, 1.355, 1.344 s
#
#   Both halves lose alone and the costs compose. Staging the batch costs
#   sixteen per cent with one subgroup, where a barrier is free and cannot
#   be blamed for it. The taller tile costs thirteen with the batch left
#   where it is.
#
#   And neither is an artefact of a cheap implementation. The first staged
#   arm wrote the tile one half-precision value at a time; rewritten to read
#   four at a time out of an f16vec4 alias of the same binding, as llama.cpp
#   reads it, the two staged arms read 1.195 and 1.419-1.445 -- the same and
#   slightly worse. Every arm answered the published digest.
#
#   What the tall tile buys is real and is not enough: a 32-row tile
#   re-reads the batch four times as often as a 128-row one, thirty-two
#   megabytes against eight for a 2048-row layer. Those re-reads are
#   second-level cache hits, and this part would rather serve them than pay
#   a shared-memory round trip and a four-wave barrier for each. That is the
#   third measurement in this file to reach that conclusion from a different
#   direction.
#
#   Kept: the shared weight tile's stride, padded. Sixteen rows are read at
#   once by a sixteen-by-sixteen load and a power-of-two stride lands all
#   sixteen in the same banks. Two sittings, alternated:
#
#     plain, stride 32    1.026, 1.029, 0.990, 1.047, 1.039, 1.052, 1.049 s
#     padded by four      1.023, 0.963, 0.984, 1.032, 1.038, 1.040, 1.034 s
#
#   Ahead in seven of seven pairs, about one per cent. And the pad swept,
#   three rounds each, so that the number is not a guess:
#
#     pad  2   1.088, 1.110, 1.097 s
#     pad  4   1.026, 1.041, 1.039 s
#     pad  8   1.034, 1.051, 1.040 s
#     pad 16   1.041, 1.049, 1.038 s
#
#   Four, eight and sixteen are the same, so what this buys is a stride that
#   is not a power of two rather than any particular bank pattern. Two is
#   worse than none, which no bank arithmetic here predicts and which is
#   recorded as not understood: a stride of sixty-eight bytes is the only
#   one of these that a sixteen-byte load cannot be assembled from, so
#   alignment rather than banking is the likely reason. Four is kept for
#   being as fast as the others and smallest.
#

# 2026-08-31, a prompt reads the weights three times where it read them
# twelve; and two careful changes to the decode that measured nothing, which
# is what found it.
#
#   The entry above leaves the matrix product 1.42 times behind and says the
#   shape is not it. So the decode was taken apart. Replacing it with a
#   constant takes the 1419-token device prompt from 1.04 s to 0.81 --
#   twenty-three per cent -- and the disassembly of the executed Q8_0 path
#   says why it looked like arithmetic:
#
#     32 WMMA, 152 VALU, 39 buffer loads, 16 ds_load_b64,
#     16 ds_store_b16, 2 ds_store_2addr_b64, 41 waits, 28 SALU
#
#   Twenty other instructions for every matrix instruction, and a shared
#   tile written one sixteen-bit value at a time.
#
#   Two changes, both of which do what they say and neither of which is
#   worth anything:
#
#     The tile held four values to an element rather than one, padded the
#     same way, so the decode's sixteen writes become four and the
#     instruction's loads read the same units. Confirmed: 16 ds_store_b16
#     and 6 ds_store_2addr_b64 become 2 ds_store_2addr_b64.
#
#     The block's scale applied to a whole four-value vector instead of
#     value by value. Confirmed: 8 v_pk_mul_f16 appear and the VALU count
#     goes 152 to 144.
#
#   Alternated, three rounds, medians of three:
#     one value at a time   1.025, 1.031, 1.046 s

#   Level. Not kept.
#
#   So the twenty-three per cent was split, because taking the decode out
#   had taken the loads that feed it out as well. Keeping the loads and the
#   scale and throwing away only the conversion chain -- wrong answers on
#   purpose, and a probe rather than a candidate:
#
#     the whole decode                        1.042, 1.052 s
#     its arithmetic gone, its loads kept     1.010, 1.012 s
#     both gone                               0.812, 0.807 s
#
#   The arithmetic is three and a half per cent and the weight reads are
#   nineteen. A hundred and twenty instructions of decoding are worth almost
#   nothing, which is why two careful attempts at them were worth nothing.
#
#   And the weights were being read twelve times. A batch is one pass over
#   the model and Max_Batch was a hundred and twenty-eight, so a 1419-token
#   prompt made twelve passes where llama.cpp, whose n_ubatch is five
#   hundred and twelve, makes three.
#
#   Swept, medians of three, the device prompt:
#     128    1.038 s
#     256    0.991 s
#     512    0.987 s
#     1024   1.026 s
#     1419   1.028 s
#     2048   1.028 s
#
#   Alternated, three rounds on the device and two on the processor:
#     device 128   1.027, 1.026, 1.036 s
#     device 512   0.961, 0.995, 0.987 s
#     cpu 128      6.440, 6.336 s
#     cpu 512      6.126, 6.248 s
#   Ahead in all five pairs. Four per cent on the device, three on the
#   processor, and the same tokens both ways.
#
#   Above five hundred and twelve it goes back, which is the part worth
#   keeping: a batch holds the activations of every position in it, and at a
#   thousand and twenty-four those stop fitting in whatever they were
#   fitting in. This is a shape with an optimum rather than a direction, and
#   the optimum is where the other runtime already had it.
#
#   Two faults found on the way, both in the machinery rather than the
#   engine, and both of the kind this file exists for:
#
#     tests speed defaulted --batch-size to a hundred and twenty-eight, a
#     copy of a default the command had just moved to five hundred and
#     twelve. A whole sitting was taken at a batch nobody would ever run
#     before the two numbers were compared. It reads the default out of the
#     request record now. Second time this tool has differed from the
#     command it publishes figures for; the first is recorded under "The
#     device backend".
#
#     Max_Batch lives in model_runner-llama.ads, which no group listed. Two
#     defaults elsewhere moved with it and those were listed, so the check
#     fired -- by luck. That file is in all five groups now, which is why
#     these fingerprints differ from the ones the gate named before the
#     lists were changed.
#
#   Also measured and not kept, from the same sitting: llama.cpp reaches its
#   speed through the cooperative matrix and not through its integer path.
#   pp1419 -ngl 99 reads 1882 t/s by default, 1800 with
#   GGML_VK_DISABLE_INTEGER_DOT_PRODUCT and 1263 with
#   GGML_VK_DISABLE_COOPMAT. Four per cent against thirty-three. A pipeline
#   per format instead of a six-way branch on a push constant is worth 0.4
#   per cent -- 1.031, 1.028 against 1.034, 1.034 -- so the branch is not a
#   cost either.
#
#   Five groups moved. One sitting, each part of it started under a load of
#   1.10:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.425 s wall, 0.065 and 0.357,
#                                      1.90 s processor
#                                      device 0.264 s wall, 0.031 and 0.233,
#                                      0.07 s processor
#     the same at --arith f32          1.360 s, 10.14 s processor
#     four tokens, cpu against
#       reference                      cpu 0.068 and 0.118
#                                      reference 5.907 and 3.932
#     110-token prompt                 cpu 0.417 s (0.415), 2.62 s processor
#                                      device 0.116 s (0.113), 0.02 s
#     1419-token prompt                cpu 6.106 s, device 0.968 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.950 s, device 1.340 s
#     twelve tokens at two bits        1.766 s (0.335 and 1.390)
#     drafted, four proposed a round   3.867 s, 24 proposed, 7 accepted
#     llama-bench 95b8e33e1, -r 3      pp110 353.57 +/- 25.04 --device none,
#                                      1606.37 +/- 55.63 -ngl 99
#                                      pp1419 266.48 +/- 2.08 --device none,
#                                      1764.35 +/- 1.34 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.53 and 56.22 beside the short
#                                      prompt, 39.08 and 55.94 beside the
#                                      long one
#                                      pp110 -ngl 0 with a device present
#                                      827.79 +/- 30.00
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b, 5f85f06494f88de4,
#   7fbdc947563a2fde.
#
#   The device reads the long prompt at 1466 tokens a second and the gap to
#   llama.cpp is 1.20, from 1.27.
#
# load:  0.79 to 2.52 across the sitting, each part started under 1.10
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, a wider tile now that a batch can fill one: measured, refused,
# and the wall behind each of the four directions named.
#
#   The entry above makes a batch five hundred and twelve, so the tile can
#   finally be wider than a hundred and twenty-eight. The argument for it is
#   arithmetic: a workgroup reads TILE_R rows of weights by the whole depth
#   to produce TILE_R by TILE_V answers, so the weight reads per answer fall
#   as the tile widens, and the weight reads are a fifth of a prompt. The
#   sweep that once called tile width neutral was taken when a batch was a
#   hundred and twenty-eight and a wider tile could never be full.
#
#   Two rounds each, medians of three, digests 1a26d24d33b8957b and
#   cbf29ce484222325 throughout:
#
#     TILE_V   1419-token prompt      110-token prompt
#       128    0.954, 0.964 s         0.114, 0.113 s
#       256    1.223, 1.176 s         0.208, 0.209 s
#       512    14.678, 14.420 s       4.713, 4.756 s
#
#   And the part says why, which is the point of taking the shader
#   statistics as well as the time:
#
#     TILE_V 128   168 registers, 0 spilled, 6 subgroups a SIMD
#     TILE_V 256   256 registers, 67 spilled, twelve kilobytes of shared
#                  memory taken to spill into, 4 subgroups a SIMD
#
#   A tile's accumulators are its registers -- TILE_R/16 by TILE_V/16 of
#   them, four apiece -- so sixteen fit and thirty-two do not. At five
#   hundred and twelve the accumulators alone want the whole register file,
#   and fifteen times is what a kernel reads when every one of them is a
#   memory access.
#
#   So the four directions out of this tile have all been measured now, and
#   they are held by different things:
#
#     wider, 256 and 512 vectors        +23 %, +1400 %   the register file
#     taller, 128 rows on four
#       subgroups                       +13 %            the barrier
#     both operands staged in shared
#       memory                          +16 %            the round trip
#     deeper, 64 and 128 columns        slower           measured earlier
#     the shared tile's stride padded   -1 %             kept
#
#   Thirty-two rows by a hundred and twenty-eight vectors on one subgroup is
#   a corner rather than a choice: more answers per workgroup needs either
#   more registers apiece or more subgroups sharing them, and this part
#   refuses both.
#
#   The loads are already as wide as they go, which was the other thing to
#   check before concluding. The executed Q8_0 loop holds thirty-four
#   buffer_load_b128 and three buffer_load_b32, and the three are the block
#   scales -- so the compiler is already merging four_words into one
#   four-word read, and there is no narrow load left to widen.
#
#   Where the prompt stands on the current kernel, two rounds each:
#
#     as it is                                       0.960, 0.960 s
#     the decode and the weight reads feeding it,
#       gone                                         0.740, 0.728 s
#     thirty-one of every thirty-two matrix
#       instructions, gone                           0.813, 0.793 s
#
#   Twenty-four per cent is the weights arriving and becoming
#   half-precision values, sixteen is the multiply, and the shape around
#   both is at a local optimum in every direction anyone has pushed it.
#
#   And where the prompt's time goes, by the engine's own accounting on the
#   same build -- the instrument costs about eighteen per cent, so read the
#   shares and not the seconds:
#
#     PROJECTING    75.6 %
#     ATTENDING     17.0 %
#     READING_OUT    2.7 %
#     NORMALIZING    2.6 %
#     ROTATING       2.0 %
#
#   Nothing kept and nothing restamped: the tree is as commit abe381d left
#   it, and no group's sources changed.
#
# load:  0.82 to 1.6 across the sitting
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, the half-precision batch turned depth-major so that a tile's
# operand would be contiguous: thirty-two per cent slower, and the layout
# was already the one the instruction wants.
#
#   The last lever on the weight-and-activation reads. The instruction's
#   second operand is sixteen depths by sixteen vectors. The batch is
#   vector-major, so reading one is sixteen runs of thirty-two bytes a whole
#   vector apart -- four kilobytes on this model. Kept depth-major, the
#   sixteen vectors a tile wants at one depth are one contiguous run of two
#   hundred and fifty-six bytes.
#
#   Contained: half_batch.comp writes that copy and matrix_product.comp is
#   its only reader, so it is two shaders and one push constant. Count is
#   pushed as the padded width rather than the real one, which the tile
#   never used.
#
#   Taken in two pieces, because a change that loses says nothing about
#   which part lost. The middle arm turns the copy and leaves the reader,
#   which computes the wrong answers on purpose -- cda91dab1bdea4fe rather
#   than 1a26d24d33b8957b -- and times the transpose by itself:
#
#     both as they were             0.968, 0.963 s
#     the copy turned only          1.052, 1.066 s
#     the reader turned with it     1.273, 1.269 s
#
#   The copy is ten per cent. "Once per product" is seven times a layer,
#   twenty-two layers, three batches -- four hundred and sixty-two
#   dispatches over a five-hundred-and-twelve by two-thousand-and-forty-
#   eight activation each -- and turning it round makes its own reads the
#   strided ones. That is the smaller surprise and it was foreseeable.
#
#   The reader is twenty-two, and that is the thing worth writing down. The
#   turned version reads contiguous memory where the original strides four
#   kilobytes, and it is much slower anyway.
#   gl_CooperativeMatrixLayoutColumnMajor is what this instruction's second
#   operand natively is on this part -- element (k, n) with k contiguous is
#   the way the lanes already hold it -- and asking for RowMajor buys a
#   rearrangement in registers that costs more than every byte of locality
#   it wins.
#
#   So the batch's vector-major layout was never an accident to be fixed. It
#   is the layout the instruction reads for free, and a four-kilobyte stride
#   that looks wrong on paper is invisible beside a transpose that is not.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit c1719b3 left it.
#
# load:  under 1.5 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, attention split four ways as llama.cpp splits it: right,
# faster on every static measure, and fourteen per cent slower.
#
#   Attention is what is left of the device prompt -- seventeen per cent of
#   it and 2.1 times behind, 0.16 s against llama.cpp's 0.078 by their own
#   per-node timings. An earlier entry priced every overhead inside this
#   kernel and found none, so what is left is structural.
#
#   Read out of ggml-vulkan.cpp rather than derived.
#   get_fa_tuning_params_coopmat1 gives this device block_rows 16 and
#   block_cols 16 * 4 -- the same sixteen-by-sixty-four tile this kernel
#   already had -- and row_split 4: four subgroups, two hundred and fifty-six
#   invocations, where this had one subgroup of sixty-four.
#
#   flash_attn_cm1.comp shows the split falling in three different places,
#   and the third is the one that makes it work:
#
#     scoring      sixteen of the tile's sixty-four cached positions
#     the softmax  four of the block's sixteen query rows
#     weighing     sixteen of the head's sixty-four components
#
#   Splitting the weighing by component means a subgroup weighs every
#   cached position of a tile against its own slice of the head, so the
#   slice it accumulates is its own from the first tile to the last and
#   nothing is reduced across the four. Only the rescale crosses, through
#   sixteen floats of shared memory -- carried, beside summed for the
#   division at the end, because the softmax owns rows and the weighing owns
#   components and neither can hand the other a register.
#
#   Built the same way here. It answers 1a26d24d33b8957b, which is the
#   published digest, and it is fourteen per cent slower. Alternated,
#   medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#
#     one subgroup      0.968, 0.951, 0.964 s
#     four subgroups    1.108, 1.099, 1.092 s
#
#   Behind in each of three rounds, and behind having won every static
#   measure the change was supposed to win:
#
#                          one subgroup   four
#     registers                     128     64
#     subgroups a SIMD                8     16
#     code                        17588   6032 bytes
#     matrix instructions a
#       subgroup a tile              32      8
#
#   Half the registers, twice the occupancy, a third of the code, a quarter
#   of the sequential arithmetic. What it buys instead is four real barriers
#   a tile where a workgroup of one subgroup needs none, and on this part
#   that trade loses.
#
#   Occupancy was not the constraint, and this is the third independent
#   measurement to say so: a 128-row product tile over four subgroups cost
#   thirteen per cent, staging both that kernel's operands cost sixteen, and
#   attention over four subgroups costs fourteen. Three kernels, three
#   shapes, one answer -- a workgroup on this part wants to be one subgroup,
#   and what it saves by being one is more than more waves in flight win
#   back.
#
#   One caution about the instrument, which this run exposed. --budget puts
#   ATTENDING at 0.241 s before the change and 0.236 s after -- no change at
#   all -- while the run it is part of is a seventh slower. Its spans are
#   host-side and the device's work runs on past them, so the phase shares
#   say where work is issued and not where the machine spends its time. The
#   end-to-end figure is the one to read, and the shares are for proportions
#   between phases rather than for judging a change.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 46e4dae left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, what attention actually costs, measured by removal rather
# than by the instrument that has been reporting it.
#
#   The entry above records a caution: --budget puts attention at 0.241 s
#   before the four-subgroup split and 0.236 after, no change, while the run
#   is a seventh slower. Its spans are host-side. Every attention figure in
#   this file came from it, so the kernel was priced again the way the
#   matrix product was -- by taking pieces out and reading the whole run.
#
#   Two rounds each, medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#
#     nothing removed                          0.957, 0.973 s
#     the scores' matrix instructions          0.959, 0.953 s
#     the weighing's matrix instructions       0.964, 0.970 s
#     the keys, a constant matrix instead      0.953, 0.958 s
#     the values                               0.943, 0.950 s
#     both                                     0.953, 0.954 s
#     the queries staged and nothing after     0.847, 0.840 s
#     the whole kernel                         0.854, 0.859, 0.853 s
#
#   Attention is 0.111 s -- a ninth of a prompt, where the instrument said a
#   sixth. Against llama.cpp's 0.078 s of FLASH_ATTN_EXT nodes that is 1.4
#   times behind rather than 2.1, and everything else in the prompt is 0.854
#   against their 0.726, which is 1.18.
#
#   So the account this file has been giving of where the device prompt's
#   gap lies was skewed: attention is a fifth of what is left rather than a
#   third, and the matrix product is more of it than the ratios said.
#
#   And nothing inside the kernel is individually attributable. Removing
#   either product's matrix instructions changes nothing. Removing the keys,
#   the values, or both changes nothing. Staging the queries is free. Only
#   removing the whole tile loop shows. That is what a loop reads like when
#   its loads, its arithmetic and its shared-memory traffic overlap and none
#   of them is the wall on its own -- the same plateau the four-subgroup
#   split found from the other direction.
#
#   The transferable part is about the instrument, not the kernel. Both
#   numbers are real measurements of different things: --budget times the
#   host's spans and says where work is issued, and removal says what the
#   machine spends. A ratio quoted against another runtime's per-node
#   timings wants the second. The phase shares stay useful for proportions
#   between phases of one build and are not to be used to judge a change --
#   which is exactly how they were used to rank attention as the next thing
#   to do.
#
#   Two probes would not run and are recorded as not run rather than
#   quietly dropped: the softmax with its exponentials replaced by a linear
#   weight, and the tile loop with everything after the scores skipped. Both
#   produce values the run rejects before it publishes anything, which is
#   the speed tool refusing to report a run that did not complete.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 6625de1 left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, where a device prompt goes with every kernel taken out in
# turn; and a layer converting four activations where it converted seven.
#
#   The entry above prices attention by removal because the instrument that
#   had been reporting it times host-side spans. So everything was priced
#   that way: each of the eight kernels a prompt runs, voided in turn by an
#   early return at the top of main -- computing nothing and leaving the
#   dispatch, the descriptors and the barriers where they were.
#
#   Two rounds each, medians of three, against a 0.964 s baseline:
#
#     matrix_product     0.323, 0.322 s      costs 0.642 s   67 %
#     attention_matrix   0.845, 0.851 s            0.116 s   12 %
#     combine            0.884, 0.889 s            0.078 s    8 %
#     half_batch         0.888, 0.888 s            0.076 s    8 %
#     norm               0.919, 0.935 s            0.037 s    4 %
#     rotate             0.954, 0.956 s            0.009 s
#     place              0.961, 0.955 s            0.006 s
#     row_product        0.957, 0.966 s            0.002 s
#
#   The eight add to 0.966 s against a prompt of 0.964, which is the check
#   that matters: nothing is unaccounted for, so this is the whole of where
#   a device prompt goes rather than a sample of it. It is also the first
#   account of it in this file that does not come from --budget.
#
#   half_batch is the surprise. It is the smallest kernel in the program --
#   a binary32 activation copied into half precision, two values a word, no
#   arithmetic -- and it costs as much as attention's keys, its values and
#   both its matrix products together.
#
#   It costs that because it ran once for every product. A layer asks for
#   seven and hands them four activations: the query, the key and the value
#   read one normalization, and both feed-forward arms read another. Three
#   of the seven copies were of what the copy before them had just made.
#
#   The engine already knows which earlier step a step reads -- it computes
#   it for the barrier that decides whether a step must wait. A tiled
#   product now remembers what the copy holds and skips the conversion when
#   the product before it converted the same answer at the same width. A
#   step of any other kind clears it, because anything else may have written
#   what the copy holds, so only adjacent products share -- which is exactly
#   the query/key/value and the two feed-forward arms.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#     converted once per product      0.944, 0.969, 0.969 s
#     converted once per activation   0.933, 0.921, 0.937 s
#   Ahead in all three rounds, 3.7 per cent, at the same tokens.
#
#   How nearly this was measured wrong, recorded because the failure is not
#   obvious. The first A/B toggled the two arms with a sed whose pattern
#   matched the one arm and not the other -- "Fresh => not Again," became
#   "Fresh => True," and then nothing matched "Again" again -- so every
#   round after the first built the same program. It read 0.960 against
#   0.949 and looked like a small loss, which would have thrown the change
#   away. What caught it was the twelve-token run coming back identical to
#   the thousandth of a second in both arms, which two different binaries do
#   not do. A toggle that edits a file in place has to be able to edit it
#   back; this one now keeps two files and copies whichever it wants.
#
#   What is left is the other four sevenths, and they need the conversion to
#   stop being a kernel: the step that produces an activation could write
#   the half-precision copy beside the binary32 one, which is what place.comp
#   already does for the cache. Then there is no second pass and no dispatch
#   to wait for.
#
#   Two groups moved. One sitting, each part started under a load of 1.10:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.461 s wall, 0.063 and 0.399,
#                                      2.06 s processor
#                                      device 0.263 s wall, 0.031 and 0.233,
#                                      0.07 s processor
#     110-token prompt                 cpu 0.427 s (0.424), 2.59 s processor
#                                      device 0.106 s (0.103), 0.02 s
#     1419-token prompt                cpu 6.068 s, device 0.946 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.929 s, device 1.373 s
#     llama-bench 95b8e33e1, -r 3      pp110 358.56 +/- 29.51 --device none,
#                                      1495.63 +/- 38.24 -ngl 99
#                                      pp1419 269.26 +/- 1.22 --device none,
#                                      1761.66 +/- 1.07 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.39 and 56.39 beside the short
#                                      prompt, 39.09 and 55.99 beside the
#                                      long one
#                                      pp110 -ngl 0 with a device present
#                                      747.03 +/- 24.13
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b.
#
#   The device reads the long prompt at 1500 tokens a second and the gap to
#   llama.cpp is 1.17, from 1.20. The processor's six-token run is high in
#   this sitting -- 0.461 s where the last two read 0.420 and 0.425 -- and
#   it started from a load of 1.07 rising to 1.39, which is the column this
#   file has always said to read with that caveat.
#
# load:  0.72 to 2.49 across the sitting
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, the half-precision conversion fused into the kernel that
# produces the activation: built, right, and level -- with the arithmetic of
# why.
#
#   The entry above leaves half_batch costing 0.047 s, made once an
#   activation rather than once a product. The way to be rid of the rest is
#   to stop it being a kernel: the step that produces an activation writes
#   the half-precision copy beside the binary32 one, which is what place.comp
#   already does for the cache. Two bytes written instead of four read and
#   two written, and no dispatch and no barrier.
#
#   norm.comp makes two of the four activations a layer converts -- the
#   query, key and value read one normalization and both feed-forward arms
#   read another -- so it went first. It writes the copy beside its own
#   answer, zeroes the positions the rounding invented, and the engine skips
#   the conversion for any product reading it.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#     the conversion a kernel of its own   0.928, 0.931, 0.943 s
#     left behind by the normalization     0.926, 0.939, 0.916 s
#   Level. Ahead in two of three and behind in one, and the same tokens.
#
#   The diagnostic is what makes this worth writing down rather than just
#   reverting. Voiding half_batch, two rounds each:
#
#     before the normalization wrote the copy   0.047 s
#     after                                     0.011 s
#
#   So the fusing removed thirty-six milliseconds of the conversion's work
#   and the run did not get thirty-six milliseconds faster. The work
#   reappeared in the normalization.
#
#   What a conversion costs is the writing, and the writing costs the same
#   wherever it is done. What fusing saves on top of that -- one read of the
#   activation, one dispatch, one barrier -- is small enough to be swallowed
#   by making a kernel that was already store-bound write two arrays instead
#   of one. The normalization's stores are strided a lane across a
#   two-thousand-wide row and it now does that twice.
#
#   The other two producers -- the combining step for the gated arm and the
#   attention kernel for the blend -- were not built. They can recover at
#   most the 0.011 s that is left and would pay the same kind of extra store
#   to do it.
#
#   One correctness trap, recorded because it was silent on the run that
#   matters and loud on the one that does not. A run whose products all take
#   the row shader has no half-precision buffer at all -- the engine sizes
#   it only when some step uses the tile -- and the binding that would carry
#   it is then the batch's own. The normalization wrote its copy through
#   that binding, and a generated token came back 695d2a94ba6aec9d where it
#   should have been 5abff916f9d83ca6, while the 1419-token prompt's digest
#   stayed right the whole time. A kernel that trusts a binding rather than
#   the caller will do this; the count came from the caller after that, with
#   zero meaning there is nothing to write.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit bc8aab5 left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, a generated token priced by removal: it is one kernel, and the
# two things that are not it are not what they looked like.
#
#   Every entry above is about a prompt. A generated token was priced the
#   same way -- each kernel voided in turn by an early return, sixty-four
#   tokens, two rounds each:
#
#     nothing voided     1.261, 1.279 s
#     row_product        0.181, 0.181 s      costs 1.089 s   86 %
#     attention          1.270, 1.276 s      nothing
#     combine            1.273, 1.269 s      nothing
#     norm               1.248, 1.249 s      nothing
#     rotate             1.270, 1.275 s      nothing
#     place              1.281, 1.282 s      nothing
#
#   A generated token is the row product and nothing else. Attention, the
#   normalizations, the joins, the rotation and the cache write all measure
#   zero at a batch of one: each is a few thousand operations against a pass
#   over every weight in the model.
#
#   Per token: 19.8 ms, of which 17.0 is the row product and 2.8 is no
#   kernel at all. llama.cpp's whole token on this device is 17.9 ms, so
#   this program's one kernel is about as fast as their entire token, and
#   the 2.8 ms around it is what puts it behind.
#
#   Two things were then tested rather than assumed, and neither is what it
#   looked like.
#
#   The kernel is at the bus and not at its instructions. It moves 1.17
#   gigabytes a token in 17.0 ms, which is 69 GB/s of a part whose peak is
#   about a hundred. A Q8_0 block is thirty-four bytes and a row begins on a
#   word, so half the blocks of every matrix straddle, and the straddling
#   path read both words of every pair -- sixteen reads for what nine words
#   cover, because the second word of one element is the first word of the
#   next. Carrying it forward reads each word once, which is a quarter off
#   every weight read this kernel makes. Bit-exact -- 448c2ed68ec342ee
#   throughout -- and level:
#
#     both words read    1.278, 1.282, 1.288 s
#     one carried        1.276, 1.282, 1.280 s
#
#   Ahead in two of three by three thousandths, which is nothing. A kernel
#   waiting on memory does not care how few instructions ask for it. Not
#   kept.
#
#   And the 2.8 ms is not the descriptors. The host writes four of them a
#   step and nine steps a layer, a hundred and ninety-eight a token, which
#   was the obvious suspect. Writing every one of them twice costs nothing:
#   1.264 s against 1.270. So what is left in the 2.8 ms is the twenty-two
#   submissions a token makes -- one a layer -- and the gap around each.
#
#   One more reading of the instrument, and the sharpest this file has.
#   --budget on a generated token says ATTENDING 75.3 per cent and
#   PROJECTING 18.7, where removal says attention nothing and the row
#   product eighty-six. The single-position path waits for a layer's fence
#   inside its attending span, so the whole layer's time lands there. The
#   instrument is not wrong; it answers where the host waits, and that is
#   where the host waits. It is not an account of where the machine spends,
#   and the entry above this one already says not to read it as one.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 69cfd1a left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, the two and a half milliseconds that are not a kernel: not the
# submissions, not the descriptors, and a walk over the vocabulary.
#
#   The entry above leaves 2.8 ms of a 19.8 ms token in no kernel and names
#   the twenty-two submissions a token makes as what is left. That was the
#   wrong suspect. Three probes, no code, and then a change somewhere else.
#
#   Not the submissions. A profile of the run with every kernel voided --
#   nothing left but the host and the queue -- puts a memory copy at the top
#   at 16.5 per cent, and it is the model going to the device once: 199
#   matrices and 1.099 GB, which --show-stats confirms with nothing given
#   back. The first token pays it and the other sixty-three do not. Under it
#   are the wait paths, which is the host correctly idle: with a layer
#   carried, every step is Kept => False and nothing is borrowed, so the
#   only Await in a token is the last layer's.
#
#   Not the descriptors either. The host writes four a step and nine steps a
#   layer, a hundred and ninety-eight a token. Writing every one of them
#   twice costs nothing: 1.264 s against 1.270.
#
#   What the profile names is Sampling.Adjusted, at 6.8 per cent of a run
#   with the kernels gone. Repeat_Penalty defaults to 1.1 over a
#   Repeat_Window of 64, and In_History -- is this token in the window? --
#   walked the ring. It is asked once for every token of the vocabulary at
#   every step, so thirty-two thousand walks of sixty-four entries is two
#   million comparisons a token, between one submission and the next.
#
#   The window is sorted and deduplicated once a token instead, in
#   Record_Token, which is the one place the history changes; In_History is
#   a binary search over it. Six comparisons rather than sixty-four, and an
#   insertion sort of at most sixty-four entries against the two million it
#   removes.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, sixty-four generated on the device:
#     the window walked    1.279, 1.282, 1.283 s
#     the window sorted    1.239, 1.251, 1.250 s
#   Ahead in all three, two and a half per cent, and bit-identical:
#   448c2ed68ec342ee both ways. The same token by the same arithmetic in a
#   different order of asking.
#
#   On the processor it is level -- 1.910 against 1.915 s -- which is what a
#   fixed cost does against a token three times as long.
#
#   And the check did not fire on it. model_runner-sampling is in no group's
#   source list, so a change that made every generated figure in this file
#   faster passed the gate in silence; the fingerprints below moved only
#   because the list was corrected. That is the second time in three days --
#   model_runner-llama.ads was the first, two entries above -- and both were
#   found by the change being faster rather than by the check. A file the
#   figures depend on that no group names is a figure with nothing watching
#   it. The sampler is in the five groups that publish a generating figure
#   now: engine, reference, device, drafting and outside.
#
#   Five groups moved. One sitting, each part started under a load of 1.10:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.422 s wall, 0.066 and 0.355,
#                                      1.94 s processor

#     the same at --arith f32          1.293 s, 9.80 s processor

#   The device reads the long prompt at 1534 tokens a second, gap 1.15, and
#   generates at 50.6, gap 1.10 -- the closest either row has been. The
#   110-token processor cell was taken twice: 0.496 s at a load of 1.08 and

# load:  0.73 to 2.58 across the sitting
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, the binary32 nobody reads: three of the four activations a
# layer converts are no longer written in it.
#
#   The entry two above has the normalization write the half-precision copy
#   beside its own answer, and it is level: the fusing added a store and
#   removed a pass, and on a part where everything is at the bus those
#   cancel. The question was the wrong one. Not "can this kernel write half
#   precision" but "does anything want the binary32", and for three of the
#   four activations a layer converts the answer is nobody:
#
#     the first normalization    read by the query, the key and the value
#     the second                 read by both feed-forward arms
#     the gated middle           read by the projection down
#
#   Every one of those readers is a tile, and a tile's operand is half
#   precision -- it reads the copy and never the original. So the original
#   was written and never read.
#
#   The engine works it out rather than being told. For each step it walks
#   the steps after it; where every step that reads it is a tiled product at
#   this shape and the host keeps none of it, the step writes only the copy.
#   Two bytes a value instead of four, into the place the conversion would
#   have put it, and the conversion does not run. Where any reader is not a
#   tile -- a rotation, a placing, a join, or a product whose rows the tile
#   shape does not take -- nothing changes, which is what the sweep checks
#   across nine architectures and fifteen formats.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#     both written      0.938, 0.940, 0.944 s
#     halves only       0.897, 0.905, 0.905 s
#   Ahead in all three, four per cent, at the same tokens. The 110-token
#   prompt goes 0.112 to 0.107.
#
#   What it removes, per layer of a five-hundred-and-twelve batch: the first
#   normalization 2 MB of binary32 write and a 6 MB conversion pass, the
#   second the same, and the gated middle 5.8 MB of write and a 17.3 MB
#   pass -- about forty megabytes a layer, and sixty-six layer-batches in a
#   1419-token prompt.
#
#   Two groups moved. One sitting, each part started under a load of 1.10:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.420 s wall, 0.063 and 0.353,
#                                      1.88 s processor
#                                      device 0.263 s wall, 0.035 and 0.227,
#                                      0.07 s processor
#     110-token prompt                 cpu 0.426 s (0.424), 2.76 s processor
#                                      device 0.108 s (0.107), 0.01 s
#     1419-token prompt                cpu 5.972 s, device 0.900 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.883 s, device 1.266 s
#     llama-bench 95b8e33e1, -r 3      pp110 350.23 +/- 28.97 --device none,
#                                      1651.33 +/- 61.30 -ngl 99
#                                      pp1419 274.09 +/- 6.31 --device none,
#                                      1763.71 +/- 3.54 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.22 and 56.25 beside the short
#                                      prompt, 38.66 and 55.80 beside the
#                                      long one
#                                      pp110 -ngl 0 with a device present
#                                      742.68 +/- 10.97
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 1a26d24d33b8957b.
#
#   The device reads the long prompt at 1577 tokens a second, gap 1.12 --
#   the closest that row has been. The 110-token processor cell was taken
#   twice again, 0.485 s at a load rising to 1.46 and 0.426 s at 1.01, and
#   the second is published.
#
#   What is left of the conversion is the attention blend, read by the
#   projection after it, which could go the same way and is one of four.

# load:  0.89 to 2.83 across the sitting
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, the fourth activation, and the conversion kernel that no
# longer runs.
#
#   The entry above takes three of the four activations a layer converts and
#   writes them only in half precision, because nothing reads the binary32.
#   The fourth is the attention blend, read by the projection after it and by
#   nothing else. The same rule, and the engine now applies it -- but only
#   where the tile kernel is the one that will attend, because the scalar
#   attention writes its blend as it always has and the engine already picks
#   between the two by that test.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#     the blend written    0.901, 0.904, 0.901 s
#     the blend halved     0.886, 0.900, 0.905 s
#   Ahead in two rounds of three and level. Predicted at rather less than one
#   per cent -- eight megabytes a layer against a prompt's nine hundred
#   milliseconds -- and measured at less than that, which is what a kernel
#   this file has already found to have no attributable piece inside it does
#   with one more store removed.
#
#   Kept anyway, for a reason that is not the time. With all four gone the
#   conversion kernel does not run. Voiding half_batch entirely -- its body
#   an immediate return -- leaves the 1419-token prompt at 0.885 s and the
#   110-token one at 0.105, and both still answer 1a26d24d33b8957b and
#   cbf29ce484222325. A kernel whose absence changes neither the answers nor
#   the time is a kernel nothing is dispatching. It stays in the program: the
#   single-product path still uses it, and so does any sequence whose
#   producer feeds something that is not a tile.
#
#   How the room was found for it, which is worth recording because the
#   obvious way was closed. The attention kernel's push block may not grow:
#   widening it by one field once corrupted this device's answers, which this
#   file records and nobody has explained. So the count of positions the copy
#   is to hold rides in the causal flag's word -- the flag in the low bit and
#   the count above it. One word, no new field, and the shader takes them
#   apart.
#
#   And one thing nearly went wrong. The first reading of this change was
#   taken from a binary that did not contain it. The build helper this work
#   uses redirected its own compiler output to nowhere, so an Ada error -- an
#   arithmetic expression on Interfaces.C.unsigned that needed a conversion
#   -- failed the build silently and the measurement ran the previous
#   executable. It read 0.893 s and every digest held, which is exactly what
#   a correct change looks like. What caught it was a second script that
#   reported the failure instead of hiding it, and then only because the
#   alternation kept failing at the same step. The helper prints its errors
#   now. A measurement harness that cannot fail loudly is a harness that will
#   publish somebody's last build.
#
#   Two groups moved. One sitting, each part started under a load of 1.05:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.418 s wall, 0.066 and 0.351,
#                                      1.90 s processor
#                                      device 0.259 s wall, 0.032 and 0.228,
#                                      0.06 s processor
#     110-token prompt                 cpu 0.439 s (0.438), 2.83 s processor
#                                      device 0.106 s (0.105), 0.02 s
#     1419-token prompt                cpu 6.064 s, device 0.892 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.887 s, device 1.283 s
#     llama-bench 95b8e33e1, -r 3      pp110 343.66 +/- 28.94 --device none,
#                                      1638.72 +/- 37.60 -ngl 99
#                                      pp1419 264.48 +/- 2.73 --device none,
#                                      1759.81 +/- 4.17 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.37 and 56.20 beside the short
#                                      prompt, 39.25 and 55.73 beside the
#                                      long one
#                                      pp110 -ngl 0 with a device present
#                                      819.78 +/- 42.18
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b.

#   The device reads the long prompt at 1591 tokens a second, gap 1.11, and
#   generates at 49.9, gap 1.12.

# load:  0.65 to 2.90 across the sitting
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, where a device prompt goes with the conversion gone, and the
# normalization's second read of its row, which is free.
#
#   The budget again, each kernel voided in turn, two rounds each against a
#   0.897 s prompt:
#
#     matrix_product     0.264, 0.259 s     costs 0.635 s   70.8 %
#     attention_matrix   0.789, 0.793 s           0.106 s   11.8 %
#     combine            0.830, 0.821 s           0.071 s    7.9 %
#     norm               0.834, 0.857 s           0.051 s    5.7 %
#     rotate             0.879, 0.880 s           0.017 s    1.9 %
#     half_batch         0.899, 0.900 s          -0.003 s
#     place              0.903, 0.898 s          -0.004 s
#
#   The five that cost anything add to 0.880 s of 0.897, and the conversion
#   kernel measures below nothing -- which is what a kernel reads when it is
#   compiled, bound, and never dispatched. That is the entry above,
#   confirmed from the other side.
#
#   Which leaves the normalization at six per cent, walking its row twice:
#   once to sum the squares and once to scale by the root of their mean. A
#   workgroup is two hundred and fifty-six lanes and a row is two thousand
#   and forty-eight values, so sixteen a lane holds the whole row and the
#   second walk need not happen. Held in an array indexed by a literal
#   inside an unrolled loop, which is what keeps it in registers rather than
#   scratch -- the shader report confirms scratch at zero -- and a row too
#   wide to hold keeps what fits and reads the rest twice as before.
#
#   Bit-exact: 1a26d24d33b8957b and 5abff916f9d83ca6 both ways, which is
#   what summing the same values in the same order gives.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#     the row read twice   0.883, 0.895, 0.902 s
#     the row kept         0.899, 0.906, 0.885 s
#   Level, and ahead in one round of three. The 110-token prompt reads 0.107
#   against 0.109 the same way round.
#
#   The shader report says why, and it is the second half of the same
#   sentence:
#
#                          registers   subgroups a SIMD
#     the row read twice          24                 32
#     the row kept                48                 20
#
#   A third of the occupancy for a read that was never going to memory. A
#   row is eight kilobytes and the kernel that wrote it ran a moment before,
#   so the second read is a cache hit: what it costs is an instruction, and
#   what holding it costs is twenty-four registers and twelve waves.
#
#   Not kept. This is the fourth measurement in this file to find that
#   occupancy is what this part is short of -- after a hundred-and-
#   twenty-eight-row product tile, its operands staged, and attention split
#   four ways -- and the first of the four where the change removed work
#   rather than adding it. The lesson is not that bigger tiles or more
#   subgroups are wrong; it is that on this part a register is worth more
#   than an instruction, and a cached read costs an instruction.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 4ac4807 left it.
#
# load:  under 1.3 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-08-31, the processor's worker loop, its two gaps, and the
# normalization's fold: three things that looked like levers and are not.
#
#   A profile of sixty-four tokens generated on the processor:
#
#     model_runner__quantization__integers__deep__rows   73.5 %
#     model_runner__backend__cpu__worker                 12.1 %
#     model_runner__backend__cpu__mat_vec                 2.7 %
#     model_runner__tensors__mat_mul_range_packed         1.8 %
#
#   Annotating the worker puts ninety-five per cent of its samples in four
#   instructions -- a load of a shared counter, a compare, a pause, a
#   decrement -- which is the Spin_Budget loop. Seven cores waiting, a
#   hundred and fifty-four times a token. It reads like pure overhead.
#
#   It is not. The budget swept, medians of three:
#     20_000   1.883, 1.923 s
#      4_000   1.927, 1.925 s
#        400   2.032, 2.057 s
#          0   2.061, 2.088 s
#   Taking the spin out costs nine per cent: the wake it avoids is worth
#   more than the core it burns.
#
#   Nor is the false sharing real, which was the other reading of the
#   annotation. Ticket and Left are adjacent fields of one record, so a
#   worker's decrement of Left as it finishes its share takes the line away
#   from the six still reading Ticket for the next job. Given each its own
#   sixty-four-byte line, aligned:
#     one line   1.907, 1.902, 1.916 s generating; 5.972 s on the prompt
#     apart      1.919, 1.880, 1.911 s generating; 6.085 s on the prompt
#   Level, and slightly worse on the prompt. Not kept.
#
#   What the workers are waiting for is the bus. The worker count swept over
#   sixty-four generated tokens:
#     3   1.875 s      6   1.913 s
#     4   1.911 s      7   1.927 s
#     5   1.916 s      8   1.918 s
#   Flat from three to eight and three the fastest. Generating reads every
#   weight once and does one multiply with each, and three cores of this
#   part saturate whatever carries them; the other four have nothing to do.
#   The spin is what makes having them cheap rather than what makes them
#   expensive.
#
#   The prompt is a different program on the same cores and it scales:
#     1   25.142 s     4   6.841 s
#     2    9.207 s     5   6.350 s
#     3    7.677 s     7   5.944 s
#   A batch shares one reading of the weights between five hundred and
#   twelve tokens, so it is arithmetic where generating is traffic. Four and
#   a quarter times on seven cores, sublinear because it is partly traffic
#   too.
#
#   So the processor's two gaps are two different problems, and this file
#   has been quoting them as one. Generating is 1.16 times behind at 39.7
#   gigabytes a second against llama.cpp's 46, on a wall both are near. The
#   prompt is 1.11 behind with real scaling left in it, and that is where
#   the row kernel is worth attacking.
#
#   And the normalization's six per cent of a device prompt is neither of
#   the two things it looked like. Its second read of the row was refused in
#   the entry above for occupancy. Its fold -- two hundred and fifty-six
#   lanes halved through eight workgroup barriers, which the shader's own
#   comment flags as what a subgroup reduction would replace -- costs
#   nothing: with the halvings removed entirely and a finite constant in
#   their place, the prompt reads 0.892 and 0.903 s against 0.892 and 0.885.
#   What is left in that kernel is the streaming and the dispatch.
#
#   One probe would not run and is recorded as not run: the fold removed
#   without a constant in its place makes a gain that is not finite, and the
#   run refuses to publish rather than reporting nonsense -- which is the
#   check working.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit dee23ec left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-01, five hundred and twelve bits on a part that has none; and
# what is left in the two elementwise kernels.
#
#   A profile of the processor reading the 1419-token prompt:
#
#     deep__rows_by_strips         62.3 %
#     kernels__head_scores          6.5 %
#     kernels__blend_run            5.6 %
#     deep__rows                    5.5 %
#     backend__cpu__worker          4.1 %
#     tensors__mat_mul_range_packed 3.4 %
#     quantize_blocks               2.0 %
#     llama__blend_exact            1.9 %
#     kernels__silu                 1.1 %
#
#   Attention is 14.1 per cent of it, which is more than the 1.11 times that
#   prompt is behind, and it is the largest thing on that side that is not
#   the hand-written strip kernel.
#
#   Nothing in this library uses a five-hundred-and-twelve-bit lane. Every
#   hand-written vector kernel is ymm, and the three files model_runner.gpr
#   gives -march=x86-64-v3 and -v4 to are quantization units.
#
#   So the blending run was widened. It weighs a whole head of values
#   against one score -- eight vfmadd231ps a position, which carry
#   sixty-five per cent of that kernel's samples by annotation -- and four
#   in zmm do the same work. Gated on Platform.Byte_Products, which is the
#   same test the v4 compilation uses and means the whole of that set.
#
#   Bit-exact, and not by luck: every component of the sum accumulates the
#   same terms in the same order, and only how many of them a register holds
#   has changed. 1a26d24d33b8957b and 3248ac1bb7011de0 throughout.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token processor prompt:
#     two hundred and fifty-six bits   6.049, 6.086, 6.199 s
#     five hundred and twelve          6.176, 6.266, 6.166 s
#   Behind in two rounds of three, about one and a half per cent.
#
#   Zen 4 executes a five-hundred-and-twelve-bit instruction on
#   two-hundred-and-fifty-six-bit hardware, two passes to the instruction,
#   so halving the instruction count leaves the cycle count where it was and
#   adds the transitions. Which is exactly why the byte dot product in the
#   quantization units is worth compiling for x86-64-v4 and this is not:
#   that is a new instruction doing four multiplies in a lane, and this is
#   only a wider lane. Not kept.
#
#   head_scores would not have gone the same way even if the lanes had paid.
#   Its annotation is led not by its multiply-adds but by six vhaddps -- the
#   horizontal reduction that ends every dot product -- and a wider lane
#   lengthens that. It would also move every digest this file publishes for
#   the processor, because a dot product's lanes are added to one another at
#   the end and their number decides the association.
#
#   The device's combining step, eight per cent of a device prompt, is
#   entirely traffic. Replacing the gated unit -- sigmoid-weighted or
#   Gaussian on one arm -- with a plain addition:
#     the unit there   0.892, 0.895 s
#     the unit gone    0.890, 0.896 s
#     the whole gone   0.823, 0.823 s
#   The arithmetic is nothing and the kernel is 0.070 s of reading two arms
#   and writing one. The only lever left is fewer bytes: the two
#   feed-forward arms exchanging half precision rather than binary32, about
#   twenty-three megabytes a layer. That changes what the program computes
#   and would be the sweep's to judge rather than a digest's.
#
#   And the processor's two small kernels are at a hundred and twenty-eight
#   bits. quantize_blocks annotates as addps, cmpleps and movups on xmm --
#   which is the SSE2 baseline this library compiles to -- and
#   mat_mul_range_packed moves its tile with movsd and movhpd, sixty-four
#   bits at a time. Neither unit gets wide flags, and that is deliberate:
#   the wide code is isolated into units chosen at run time so that the
#   binary runs on a processor that has none of it. What those two want is
#   the treatment the row kernels already have, a wide variant beside the
#   baseline -- and the measurement above says to widen them to two hundred
#   and fifty-six bits and stop.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit f6c5ae1 left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-01, the activation quantizer widened: seven tenths of a per cent,
# and it does not answer the same.
#
#   The entry above finds quantize_blocks running on a hundred and
#   twenty-eight bit lanes -- addps, cmpleps and movups on xmm -- because
#   its unit compiles at the baseline this library targets, while only three
#   files in the project file carry -march=x86-64-v3 or -v4.
#
#   Widened as a probe rather than as a change: the whole unit compiled for
#   x86-64-v3, which is not shippable, because the binary would then fault
#   on a processor without that set and the whole point of isolating the
#   wide code into units chosen at run time is that it does not. The probe
#   answers what the width is worth before four files of plumbing are
#   written to get it safely.
#
#   It is worth seven tenths of one per cent. The wall clock read 5.809 s
#   against about 6.05, which looks like four -- but the processor's prompt
#   column swings by more than that between sittings, and this file has said
#   so for months. The profile is the honest measure:
#
#                          baseline   widened
#     quantize_blocks         2.0 %    1.27 %
#     everything else                 unmoved
#
#   And it does not answer the same. The 1419-token prompt comes back
#   b8887185cdd328a7 where it has always been 1a26d24d33b8957b, and
#   reverting the switch restores it.
#
#   Nothing in that unit reduces across lanes: its only accumulation is an
#   Integer_32 total, which is exact in any order, and Block_Extent takes a
#   maximum, which is associative. So what changed is the rounding.
#   Real'Rounding is ties away from zero; the baseline reaches that by
#   adding and truncating and the vectorised form reaches for a rounding
#   instruction, and a value landing exactly on a half then goes the other
#   way. Three million activations a batch is enough for that to show, and
#   it is a reminder that "no reduction, therefore bit-exact" is not a proof
#   -- it was the reasoning that made this look safe to widen.
#
#   Seven tenths of a per cent, four files of plumbing, and a different
#   answer. Not built.
#
#   Worth setting beside the entry above it, because the two are the same
#   lesson from opposite ends. Widening the blending run was bit-exact and
#   slower. Widening the quantizer is faster and not bit-exact. Neither is
#   what a wider lane was expected to give, and in both the reason is a
#   property of the instruction rather than of the lane: one is
#   double-pumped on this part, and the other rounds differently.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 6dbbf11 left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-01, the tile write-back widened: bit-exact, level, and a strided
# read is why.
#
#   The entry above widens the activation quantizer and finds seven tenths
#   of a per cent and a different answer. The other half of that item is
#   mat_mul_range_packed, three and a half per cent of a processor prompt,
#   whose annotation is movsd and movhpd -- sixty-four bits at a time.
#
#   It narrows a tile of binary64 accumulators into binary32 and lays them
#   out the way the target wants them. Unlike Real'Rounding there is no
#   emulation that can differ: a double narrowed to a float is
#   round-to-nearest-even, which is one instruction at any width. So this
#   one is bit-exact, and the probe confirms it -- 1a26d24d33b8957b
#   throughout, where the quantizer's probe moved to b8887185cdd328a7.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token processor prompt, the unit
#   compiled for x86-64-v3 as a probe:
#     baseline   5.786, 6.137, 6.220 s
#     wide       6.266, 6.053, 6.218 s
#   Ahead in one round of three. Level.
#
#   The profile says why: the share moves 3.26 per cent to 3.04, which is
#   nothing. The loop writes the target contiguously and reads the tile with
#   a stride of Count, and a wider lane does not help a strided read -- the
#   gather on this part is slow enough that the compiler keeps the paired
#   sixty-four-bit moves whichever instruction set it is given.
#
#   One of the two sides has to be strided. Which one is the subject of "A
#   tile written the way the target is laid out" in the README, where the
#   loops were swapped so that the target is the contiguous side; widening
#   the lanes does not revisit that choice, it only makes the contiguous
#   side wider while the strided side still sets the pace.
#
#   And the reading that started this was noise. The first measurement of
#   the widened unit read 5.648 s against about 6.05 and looked like six and
#   a half per cent; three alternated rounds put it at nothing. The
#   processor's prompt column swings five per cent between sittings, which
#   this file has recorded for months, and this is the third time in this
#   session that a single reading of it has pointed the wrong way -- after
#   the descriptor probe and the first blend-widening reading.
#
#   So both halves of that item are answered: the quantizer is worth seven
#   tenths of a per cent and does not answer the same, and the write-back is
#   bit-exact and worth nothing. Neither is built.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 4d7ec90 left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-01, the feed-forward arms in half precision: worth five and a half
# per cent, and the buffer they need to be correct in costs twenty-two.
#
#   The two arms of a gated feed-forward are read by the step that combines
#   them and by nothing else, and that step reads half precision already. So
#   the products that make them need not write binary32 -- the rule of "the
#   binary32 nobody reads" one step further: a product whose only reader is
#   a combining step that reads halves.
#
#   Built. matrix_product converts its accumulator and stores it where the
#   answer is wanted; combine reads both arms out of the half-precision
#   buffer rather than through bindings nought and one; the engine works out
#   which products qualify by walking the steps after each one, as it
#   already does for the tiles.
#
#   Alternated, medians of three, the 1419-token device prompt:
#     arms in binary32   0.904, 0.896, 0.901 s
#     arms in halves     0.848, 0.853, 0.862 s
#   Five and a half per cent, ahead in all three rounds. The answers change,
#   as they must: the gate's activation is computed from half-precision
#   values, so this is the sweep's to judge rather than a digest's.
#
#   And the answers changed every run. Three runs of one binary gave
#   2b4795a8775ecaa4, 70a2599caf019dd7 and 47d5e948c41ae62a. That is not a
#   wrong answer, it is a race, and it was caught only because the measuring
#   tool prints a digest for every run rather than a pass or a fail. A
#   conformance bound would have accepted all three.
#
#   The race is the layout. The half-precision buffer held one region and
#   everything wrote at the front of it, which was safe while only one
#   answer was ever live at a time. Two arms need two places -- but three
#   answers are live at once, not two: the normalization both arms read is
#   still sitting in the first region when the first arm writes over it, and
#   whether the second arm reads it before or after is timing.
#
#   So three regions. And three regions is where it dies. The buffer goes
#   from eleven and a half megabytes to seventeen, and that alone costs
#   twenty-two per cent. The control is exact: the arms left in binary32,
#   the shaders and the engine as they were, and nothing changed but the
#   bytes asked of Take --
#
#     one region and a half (11.5 MB)   0.901 s, digest 1a26d24d33b8957b
#     three regions          (17.3 MB)  1.102, 1.106 s, same digest
#
#   -- and the same layout at two regions read 0.848 with the arms halved.
#   It is not the regions and not the arms; it is the size of the
#   allocation.
#
#   Why a seventeen-megabyte buffer should cost a fifth of a prompt on a
#   part already holding 1.1 gigabytes of weights is not explained here.
#   Something about where an allocation of that size lands -- a memory type,
#   a page size, a translation structure -- but this measures rather than
#   explains, and it is recorded because it is repeatable and controlled and
#   larger than anything else in this section.
#
#   Five and a half per cent for the arms, twenty-two for the room they need
#   to be correct in. Not kept.
#
#   Nothing kept, nothing restamped: the tree is as commit 09f195f left it.
#
# load:  under 1.4 throughout
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-01, the feed-forward arms in half precision, kept -- and the
# control in the entry above corrected.
#
#   The entry above records this change as refused, on a control that said
#   the buffer it needs costs twenty-two per cent. That control was wrong.
#   It was two readings at --repeats 1 with no test of what else the machine
#   was doing, and it does not reproduce.
#
#   The allocation swept, five sizes, medians of three, two rounds, with the
#   arms left in binary32 throughout so that nothing but the bytes asked of
#   Take differs:
#
#     one region     0.893, 0.894 s
#     two            0.882, 0.894 s
#     three          0.891, 0.881 s
#     four           0.894, 0.910 s
#     six            0.890, 0.894 s
#
#   Flat. The size of that allocation costs nothing.
#
#   So all four cells were measured together, alternated, each waiting for
#   the machine to fall below 1.05:
#
#     binary32 arms, one region     0.881, 0.891 s   published digests
#     binary32 arms, three regions  0.893, 0.885 s   published digests
#     halved arms, two regions      0.845, 0.847 s   a different answer
#                                                    every run
#     halved arms, three regions    0.851, 0.842 s   published digests
#
#   The three-region form is as fast as the racing two-region one and gives
#   the same tokens. Kept.
#
#   What the two-region form was doing is worth keeping too. Two arms need
#   two places, but three answers are live at once: the normalization both
#   arms read is still sitting in the first region when the first arm writes
#   over it, and whether the second arm reads it before or after is timing.
#   It showed as d2c51a052ab789a2, e7fae034fbfe106e, 2b4795a8775ecaa4,
#   70a2599caf019dd7 and 47d5e948c41ae62a from one binary -- caught only
#   because the tool prints a digest for every run rather than a pass or a
#   fail. A conformance bound would have accepted all five.
#
#   And the kept form gives the published digests, which is not luck and not
#   an accident of rounding either. The digest is of the generated text. The
#   arms crossing in half precision moves the logits in their last bits and
#   moves no argmax, so the text is the same; the sweep is what judges the
#   logits, and it reads 28344 sequences across nine architectures and
#   fifteen formats with none outside tolerance. That is also what says the
#   racing version's digests were a race and not rounding: rounding does not
#   change the text and garbage does.
#
#   Three readings taken at --repeats 1 have now pointed the wrong way in
#   this work -- the descriptor probe, the first blend widening, and this
#   control -- and this one nearly cost a change that is correct and worth
#   four and a half per cent. Alternating the arms, medians of three, and
#   waiting for the machine is not a formality, and a control deserves it as
#   much as a candidate does.
#
#   Two groups moved. One sitting, each part started under a load of 1.05:

# 2026-09-01, both of attention's loops moved into the kernel that scores.
#
#   A profile of the long prompt on the processor puts head_scores at 6.7 per
#   cent of it. Forty-six per cent of that kernel's samples were not in the
#   run: ten arguments, six reach comparisons, an index check on each of
#   three arrays every time an address was taken, and a frame -- in front of
#   sixty-four multiply-adds, because the caller walks a block of eight keys
#   and one head at a time and that is one turn of the loop inside.
#
#   Head_Scores_Across takes the head range and the whole key run, proves the
#   reach once, and issues the run in place. The run is a constant both
#   procedures share, so it is the same instructions in the same order, and
#   the scores are bit for bit what they were. Head_Scores still does one
#   head and takes the tail and every host without the lanes.
#
#   Counted rather than timed, two alternated rounds:
#
#     a head at a time    1032457207147 and 1032422518763 instructions
#                          380124653266 and  378714944135 cycles
#     the range at once    991366405630 and  991291133539 instructions
#                          375974170333 and  370745329887 cycles
#
#   Four per cent of the instructions and 1.6 per cent of the cycles, both
#   reproducing to five figures. Nine wall-clock runs alternated over three
#   rounds read 6.057 s against 5.952 -- 1.7 per cent, and inside the spread
#   on its own. The counters are what settle this one.
#
#   Inside the kernel the multiply-adds went from 39 per cent of the samples
#   to 51 and the prologue from 46 to 30. The run is at both walls at once,
#   sixty-four loads and sixty-four multiply-adds to a block on a part that
#   does two of each a cycle, so there is nothing left to trade.
#
#   src/library/model_runner-kernels.ads was in no group's source list, and a
#   change to it would have passed the gate in silence. It is in the five
#   groups that name the body now, which is why their stamps move further
#   than the change alone would move them.
#
#   Five groups moved. Two sittings, and the reason for two is here:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.414 s wall, 0.060 and 0.352,
#                                      1.84 s processor; loading 0.075 s
#                                      device 0.267 s wall, 0.030 and 0.236,

#                                      684.33 +/- 51.81 -ngl 0
#                                      tg64 39.19 +/- 0.29 and 55.80 +/- 0.05

#   The two 1419-token rows reached the end of that sitting at a load of
#   three, so both engines were taken again afterwards in one quiet window:
#
#     1419-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 5.883 s wall, 5.450 evaluating,
#                                      35.81 s processor, load 0.45

#   Eleven per cent separates that prompt read settled and quiet from the
#   same prompt read at a load of three, and about four separates settled
#   from a part warm from rebuilding. The alternation says which arm is
#   faster; only the settled sitting may be published as a figure.

#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b, 5f85f06494f88de4,
#   7fbdc947563a2fde, 4b6e8e99ae285b2a.

#   The processor reads the long prompt at 260.4 tokens a second against
#   llama.cpp's 270.0, which is a gap of 1.04 -- the closest either side of
#   this comparison has been at either length.
