# load:  0.45 to 3.72 across the two sittings
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, and Q4_K_M and Q2_K beside it
#
# 2026-09-01, four measurements of the generating row kernel, none kept, and
# a diagnosis corrected.
#
#   The list this came from said the generating kernel copies and biases
#   every weight before multiplying it. It does not. The staging copy is in
#   the four-row insertion, and a generated token does not go there: deleting
#   that loop outright moved the instruction count of a sixty-four token run
#   by four hundredths of a per cent -- 81216 against 81181 million -- which
#   is how a path is proved cold. What a token runs is Rows_Singly, and that
#   one already reads the weights where the file holds them, keeps the
#   accumulator in a register from a row's first block to its last, and
#   issues eleven instructions for every thirty-two multiply-adds.
#
#   What the counters do say is that the generating side is not waiting for
#   the bus. Sixty-four tokens: 81.2 billion instructions in 35.5 billion
#   cycles, which is 2.29 a cycle. Reading a gigabyte of weights a token at
#   about 31 GB/s on a part that does far more, at that rate of issue, is a
#   program limited by what it executes rather than by what it fetches. This
#   file has said the other thing for several sittings.
#
#   Four things were then measured. All four are refused.
#
#   The block scale, decoded by the hardware rather than by Ada. Scale_At
#   builds a sixteen-bit number out of two byte loads, a shift and an or
#   before vcvtph2ps can start -- six instructions in a function every block
#   of every row calls. vpinsrw takes the two bytes at the address directly,
#   which is three:
#
#     a head at a time     81220, 81274, 81204 million instructions
#     the pair loaded      74404, 74411, 74396
#
#   Eight and four tenths per cent of every instruction a generated token
#   executes, reproducing to five figures, and the tokens are identical
#   because it is the same convert of the same two bytes. It is not faster.
#   Q8_0 cycles read 35.50 against 34.77 in one sitting and 35.61 against
#   35.85 in the next, which is noise around zero, and Q4_K generation is
#   clearly worse: 9.86 billion cycles against 14.90, and 0.40 s generating
#   against 0.69. The two byte loads issue in parallel with everything
#   around them; vpinsrw is a load and an insert into one register, and the
#   convert waits behind both. Fewer instructions on a longer chain.
#
#   The four kilobytes of `rep stosq` at the top of that kernel. Row_Scale
#   has room for a thousand blocks and is zeroed on entry to set the
#   hundred and seventy-six a row will overwrite; the instruction was among
#   the ten hottest in a profile of a generated token. Alternated, three
#   rounds: 1.893 s generating against 1.870, and Q4_K level. One and two
#   tenths per cent, under the floor.
#
#   The bias correction as an integer rather than a sum. The insertion's
#   unsigned operand is the weight byte with its sign bit flipped, and what
#   that adds comes back out as a running binary64 sum over every block of
#   every row -- seven instructions a block, and removing it outright takes
#   twelve per cent off the whole run. It need not be a sum at all: the
#   correction is an integer smaller than a million, it depends on the
#   activation block and not on the row, and the lane the multiply-add
#   accumulates into can start at it instead of at zero, which is a load
#   where the insertion had a xor.
#
#     base                 81220, 81274, 81204 million instructions
#                          35499, 36155, 35264 million cycles
#     the pair loaded       and the correction folded in
#                          66191, 66226, 66165 million instructions
#                          35432, 35420, 34861 million cycles
#
#   Eighteen and a half per cent of the instructions and nothing at all on
#   the clock -- the cycles read one and nine tenths per cent worse than the
#   pair-loaded arm alone. The xor it replaced is a zeroing idiom, which
#   costs no execution at all, and the load it becomes goes into the chain
#   the multiply-add is already waiting on. It moves the answers as well
#   (3740ed87be385f2d against 3248ac1bb7011de0, and the suite passes at 294
#   of 294): the correction becomes exact where it was a rounded sum. Being
#   more nearly right for nothing is not a reason to change published
#   digests.
#
#   Nothing is kept and no figure moves. What is worth keeping is the shape
#   of the answer: three of the four cut instructions by four to eighteen
#   per cent and none of them cut cycles, on a kernel issuing 2.29
#   instructions a cycle. There is no front-end slack left in it. The next
#   thing to try on this side has to shorten a dependency chain or move
#   fewer bytes, not execute fewer instructions -- and the entry two above
#   this one, which cut four per cent of the instructions and 1.6 per cent
#   of the cycles, is the same lesson read from the other end.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 and Q4_K_M
#
# 2026-09-01, the block scale in three instructions, in the one kernel that
# can spend them -- and two corrections to the entry above.
#
#   The entry above refused the pair-loading decode outright. That was one
#   refusal too many. It is worth eight and four tenths per cent of every
#   instruction a generated token executes, and where it hurts is Q4_K,
#   which does not share a code path with the kernel that gains. Rows_Singly
#   is Q8_0 and nothing else -- the block width is a literal 34 in its
#   insertion -- so the decode goes there as Scale_Pair_At and the shared
#   Scale_At is left alone.
#
#     two byte loads, a shift, an or   4.594, 4.530, 4.597 s at one worker
#                                      1.891, 1.879, 1.898 s at seven
#     the pair in one instruction      4.410, 4.354, 4.412 s at one worker
#                                      1.864, 1.876, 1.857 s at seven
#
#   Four per cent at one worker and 1.4 at seven, every reading of one arm
#   below every reading of the other, and the same tokens: it is the same
#   convert of the same two bytes. Q4_K reads 0.400 against 0.405, which is
#   the noise, because it never enters this kernel.
#
#   The first correction. The entry above says the generating side is not
#   waiting for the bus, on an instruction-per-cycle figure of 2.29. That
#   figure is an average over cycles that include seven workers spinning in
#   a four-instruction loop waiting for the next job, and that loop issues
#   at nearly four a cycle. Asked by worker count instead:
#
#     one worker      4.575 s generating, 16 GB/s, 3.46 instructions a cycle
#     three workers   1.894 s, 40 GB/s
#     seven workers   1.893 s, 40 GB/s
#
#   Issue-bound on one core and bus-bound on three, with the corner at
#   three -- which is what this file said before that entry and what it says
#   again. It also explains the entry above rather than contradicting it:
#   four changes cut instructions and none cut cycles because at seven
#   workers instructions are free. The one kept here is the same change
#   measured where they are not.
#
#   The second correction, and it is about method. The entry above published
#   the 1419-token processor prompt at 260.4 tokens a second and a gap of
#   1.04, from a retake that read 5.450 s. Every other reading of that same
#   binary that day lies between 5.9 and 6.1: nine alternated readings in
#   the A/B beside it, three in the sitting, and this sitting's own quiet
#   retake at 6.071. The 5.450 was a median of three taken inside one
#   unusually good window, and a median of three is no defence against the
#   window itself. Readings have to be spread across windows. The row now
#   says 233.7 and 1.16, and the rule this file already had -- alternate to
#   say which arm is faster, settle to say what the figure is -- needs a
#   third clause: take the figure in more than one sitting before publishing
#   it.
#
#   Blend_Run, two positions a turn, measured and refused. Its inner loop is
#   eight fused multiply-adds behind a broadcast, two pointer advances, a
#   decrement and a branch; two positions share one set of four and the
#   second advance becomes a lea of twice the stride, which is twenty-two
#   instructions where two turns were twenty-six. Bit for bit the same
#   answer.
#
#     a position a turn    331067, 331087, 331150 million instructions
#                          124327, 125318, 127700 million cycles
#     two a turn           329823, 329857, 329826 million instructions
#                          125319, 124914, 126582 million cycles
#
#   Thirty-eight hundredths of a per cent of the instructions and nothing on
#   the clock. The arithmetic said so first: fifteen per cent of a kernel
#   that is 5.7 per cent of a prompt is under a per cent of the prompt, and
#   this machine gives back more than half of any instruction cut.
#
#   Six groups moved. The sitting, and a quiet window for the long prompt:
#
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.417 s wall, 0.056 and 0.357,
#                                      1.85 s processor; loading 0.070 s
#                                      device 0.263 s wall, 0.033 and 0.231,
#                                      0.08 s processor
#     the same at --arith f32          1.516 s, 11.13 s processor
#     the same, twelve-token, device   cpu 0.410 s (0.059 and 0.352), 1.83 s

# Moved here from docs/measured-figures.txt when that file passed

# here: it is the prose that moved.

# 2026-09-01, three items priced, none kept, and one of the three premises
# was wrong.
#
#   The device prompt has no fixed cost worth finding. The list said a
#   110-token device prompt carries about thirty-five milliseconds that a
#   1419-token one carries too, from the two lengths not lying on a line
#   through the origin. Voided every compute shader at once, that prompt
#   reads 0.008 s against 0.104 -- so ninety-two per cent of it is in the
#   shaders and eight milliseconds is everything else. There is no fixed
#   cost outside the work.
#
#   Where the short prompt's gap is instead: matrix_product costs 0.088 s of
#   the 0.104 at 110 tokens and 0.604 of 0.850 at 1419, which is 0.80
#   milliseconds a token against 0.425. The tile product is one and nine
#   tenths times as expensive per token at the shorter length, and that is
#   the whole of the 1.51 against 1.11.
#
#   Not the batch, which was the first thing asked. The long prompt in one
#   batch of 1536 reads 0.845 s against 0.831 in three of 512; at 256 it is
#   0.852, at 128 0.919, at 64 1.549. The short prompt is one batch at every
#   size down to 128 and reads 0.102 to 0.103 throughout. Coarser is
#   slightly worse and finer is much worse, and neither is the fixed cost.
#
#   And a limit of the method itself, worth writing down. Voiding one kernel
#   at a time in the 110-token prompt:
#
#     none voided        0.104 s        rotate + place         0.075 s
#     matrix_product     0.016          + norm                 0.072
#     attention_matrix   0.072          five small together    0.069
#     combine            0.073          all six                0.008
#     norm               0.071
#     rotate             0.074
#     place              0.076
#
#   Each of the five small kernels appears to cost about thirty-two
#   milliseconds alone and all five together cost thirty-five. That cannot
#   be true of any of them. At a prompt this short, removing one kernel
#   stops measuring that kernel -- place costs nothing at all at 1419
#   tokens and appears to cost twenty-eight milliseconds here -- and only
#   the two ends of the table can be trusted. Every earlier budget in this
#   file was taken on the long prompt, where the same kernels do add up, and
#   that is the length to take it at.
#
#   The strip kernel's scale table, priced and refused. It holds the weight
#   scale times the activation scale, one number for every row, block and
#   vector of a panel, and the insertion reads it as the broadcast operand
#   of its fused multiply-add. Removing the multiply and the load that feeds
#   it -- a build that answers wrongly, and only a ceiling -- reads:
#
#     as it is           5.842, 5.925, 5.853 s
#                        329821, 329989 million instructions
#     multiply removed   5.526, 5.686, 5.650 s
#                        320482, 320493, 320499 million instructions
#
#   Three and a half per cent of the prompt and 2.9 of its instructions.
#   Neither route to it works.
#
#   Reading the eight activation scales once for a block rather than once
#   for each of the panel's four rows is bit for bit the same and slower:
#   6.083 s against 5.929, and a Q4_K prompt 0.621 against 0.578. GNAT was
#   already doing it; materializing the eight as an aggregate is what the
#   change actually added.
#
#   Moving the multiply into the insertion does not pay either, and the
#   arithmetic says so without a build. The table would go entirely: scale
#   the converted dot product by the weight scale and let the multiply-add
#   broadcast the activation scale straight out of the per-call table. But
#   the insertion holds two rows and eight vectors, so that is sixteen extra
#   vmulps for every block, against three or four instructions the table
#   costs for the same block -- and it would move every processor digest,
#   because (W x V) . dot rounds the scales together and V . (W . dot)
#   rounds the scaled dot. Four times the work to be less exact.
#
#   The two small prompt kernels are not overheads at all. quantize_blocks
#   reads 2.1 per cent of a prompt in a profile; removing it takes the
#   prompt from 5.888 s to 6.734, because without a quantized activation
#   the integer path refuses and the floating-point one runs instead. It is
#   a purchase, not a cost. mat_mul_range_packed reads 3.4 per cent;
#   removing it takes the prompt to 1.541 s, because it is the procedure
#   that dispatches the whole product rather than a kernel beside it, and
#   its share is only the part the strip kernels do not inline. Neither is
#   a target and neither was ever three per cent of anything.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0 and Q4_K_M
#
# 2026-09-01, the feed-forward's activation was running on one core, and the
# processor's long prompt is level with llama.cpp.
#
#   A profile sorted by thread rather than by symbol. silu and multiply --
#   the gate unit and the multiply by the up projection -- appear on the main
#   task and on no worker at all, where every other hot symbol appears on all
#   eight. They are 1.18 and 0.59 per cent of the samples collected, and at
#   35.66 seconds of processor time in 5.93 of wall that is about 0.63
#   seconds of the prompt on one core of eight.
#
#   Three of this block's loops over a batch already went to the pool. This
#   is the fifth and was never named: a batch is five hundred and twelve
#   positions of five thousand six hundred and thirty-two numbers, twenty-two
#   times over. Elementwise like the others, so a share is the same
#   arithmetic in the same order and the answers do not move.
#
#     on one core    5.882, 6.009, 5.930 s evaluating the long prompt
#                    0.460, 0.475 s at 110 tokens
#     shared out     5.350, 5.355, 5.391 s
#                    0.394, 0.465, 0.448 s
#
#   Nine and seven tenths per cent, every reading of one arm below every
#   reading of the other, and 1a26d24d33b8957b both ways. The device row does
#   not move -- 0.854, 0.875, 0.896 against 0.854, 0.864, 0.889 -- because a
#   device takes the whole gated block at once and never runs this loop.
#
#   What is worth keeping is the sort order. Every profile in this file
#   before this one was sorted by symbol, and by symbol these two kernels
#   read 1.8 per cent of a prompt and look like rounding. A symbol's share of
#   the samples is its share of the time only when the samples are spread
#   evenly across the threads, and a worker pool is exactly the arrangement
#   in which they are not.
#
#   Five groups moved. One sitting, gated to a load under 0.7 and a settled
#   part before the first reading rather than only cooled:
#
#     1419-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 5.695 s wall, 5.273 evaluating,
#                                      37.00 s processor, load 0.80
#                                      device 1.197 s wall, 0.893 evaluating,
#                                      0.27 s processor
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.412 s wall, 0.060 and 0.355,
#                                      1.86 s processor; loading 0.070 s
#                                      device 0.268 s wall, 0.034 and 0.234,
#                                      0.08 s processor
#     the same at --arith f32          1.540 s, 11.16 s processor
#     the same, twelve-token, device   cpu 0.417 s (0.058 and 0.357), 1.90 s
#     110-token prompt                 cpu 0.382 s (0.381), 2.82 s processor
#                                      device 0.105 s (0.104), 0.01 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.964 s (1.902), device 1.320 s
#                                      (1.273)
#     seven workers against fifteen    0.412 s and 1.86 s processor against
#                                      0.546 s and 2.94 s

#     four tokens, cpu                 0.061 s prompt, 0.119 s generating
#     four tokens, reference           6.094 s prompt, 4.024 s generating
#     drafting                         model 0.425 s, two-bit 1.904 s,
#                                      drafted 3.891 s, 24 proposed 7
#                                      accepted

#     llama-bench 95b8e33e1, -r 3      pp110 311.68 +/- 23.50 --device none,
#                                      1650.10 +/- 22.64 -ngl 99,
#                                      677.85 +/- 28.91 -ngl 0
#                                      pp1419 269.84 +/- 0.81 --device none,
#                                      1751.90 +/- 0.72 -ngl 99
#                                      tg64 39.24 +/- 0.03 and 55.36 +/- 0.48
#
#   The processor reads the long prompt at 269.1 tokens a second against
#   llama.cpp's 269.84, which is a gap of 1.00 -- level, for the first time
#   on either side of this comparison, where it was 2.7 when this work began.
#   The reading is checked against the rule the entry before this one added:
#   5.273 s here sits inside the 5.350 to 5.391 the alternated A/B read in a
#   different window, so it is a sitting and not a window.
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b, 5f85f06494f88de4,

# load:  0.80 at the first reading, 4.78 by the last
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, and Q4_K_M and Q2_K beside it
#
# 2026-09-01, two accumulators in the generating kernel: seventeen per cent
# at one worker, and refused because of what it does to drafting.
#
#   First the cheap probe, and it is refuted. The generating side reads
#   1.17 gigabytes a token through four-kilobyte pages, which is two hundred
#   and eighty-five thousand of them, and a page walk apiece would be a
#   plausible reason to be at 40 GB/s where llama.cpp is at 46. Counted:
#   11.1 million second-level TLB misses over sixty-four tokens, a hundred
#   and seventy-three thousand a token, which at twenty-five cycles each is
#   about eight tenths of one per cent of the run. It is not the page
#   tables.
#
#   What it is, below three workers, is the accumulator. Rows_Singly keeps
#   one binary32 accumulator for a row and adds a block into it with a fused
#   multiply-add, so consecutive blocks are a dependent chain four cycles
#   long apiece. Two accumulators, even blocks into one and odd into the
#   other, folded once at the end:
#
#     one worker      4.447, 4.388, 4.367 s generating
#                     3.641, 3.672 s with two
#     two workers     1.947, 1.926 s against 1.867, 1.842
#     three workers   1.883, 1.825 s against 1.801
#     five workers    1.867, 1.858 s against 1.834, 1.824
#     seven workers   1.871, 1.888, 1.878 s against 1.998, 1.856, 1.845
#
#   Seventeen per cent at one worker, four at two, three at three, two at
#   five and one at seven. The fewer the workers the more it is worth, which
#   is the same shape every instruction cut on this side has had: the chain
#   is the wall while one core is running and the bus is the wall once three
#   are.
#
#   Refused, and the reason is not the speed. It moves two digests -- the
#   twelve-token run from 33f48397f89839f6 to 5abff916f9d83ca6 and the
#   sixty-four-token one from 3248ac1bb7011de0 to 448c2ed68ec342ee -- and
#   both of those are answers this program already gives elsewhere: they are
#   what --arith f32 says and what the device says. So the two-accumulator
#   sum is nearer the exact one, not further.
#
#   But it moves only the single-vector kernel, and the batch kernel cannot
#   follow. Rows_By_Strips holds eighteen accumulators in ymm8 to ymm25, one
#   for every row and vector of its tile, and doubling them does not fit in
#   thirty-two registers. The two kernels then sum a row's blocks in
#   different orders -- and drafting depends on their agreeing. In the
#   sitting taken with the change in, the drafted twelve-token run printed
#   33f48397f89839f6 and the undrafted one 5abff916f9d83ca6: a draft
#   proposes a token at a time and the model checks several at once, so one
#   answer comes from the strip kernel and the other from the single-vector
#   one. The guarantee this repository states -- that a drafted run produces
#   exactly what the model would have produced alone -- was false in a
#   published figure.
#
#   Nothing caught it. The suite passed 294 of 294 and the sweep read 28344
#   sequences with none outside tolerance; the test that runs a prompt with
#   and without a draft did not diverge on its own model. What caught it was
#   the sitting's own table, printing the two digests one line apart, which
#   is a person reading rather than a check firing -- the second time in this
#   file that has been the case.
#
#   So the constraint is worth writing down, because it is not obvious and
#   nothing enforces it: Rows_Singly and Rows_By_Strips must sum a row's
#   blocks in the same order and the same precision. They do today because
#   both were written with one accumulator a row, and either one changing
#   alone breaks drafting.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, with Q2_K as the draft
#
# 2026-09-01, three more attempts on the two remaining gaps, all level.
#
#   The generating side, after the page tables and the accumulator. A
#   software prefetch of the weights, one instruction in an eleven-
#   instruction loop, at two distances:
#
#     as it is         1.869, 1.906, 1.876 s generating
#     256 bytes ahead  1.870, 1.876, 1.866
#     512 bytes ahead  1.871, 1.870, 1.872
#
#   Level at both distances, and bit for bit the same answer. The rows a
#   worker walks are contiguous and the hardware prefetcher has them
#   already; there was nothing for the instruction to ask for.
#
#   So the generating gap -- 40 GB/s against llama.cpp's 46 for the same
#   bytes -- is now three things it is not. Not the page tables, at eight
#   tenths of one per cent. Not the accumulator chain, which is worth
#   seventeen per cent at one worker and one at seven and cannot be taken
#   anyway. Not the prefetcher.
#
#   The device's combining step, and a piece of reasoning that was wrong.
#   It costs seventy-one milliseconds of a 1419-token device prompt, moves
#   about 1.06 gigabytes, and so runs at sixteen gigabytes a second on a
#   part that does several times that. An invocation read two halves and
#   wrote one -- two bytes a lane -- and that looked like the reason.
#
#   It is not. Four values to an invocation, through f16vec4 and vec4
#   aliases of the same bindings with a guard for a shape that would not
#   line up, and a dispatch a quarter the size:
#
#     one value an invocation   0.844, 0.845, 0.850 s
#     four                      0.844, 0.846, 0.843
#
#   Level, and the generating row and the short prompt level with it. The
#   mistake was thinking of a lane's access width as the unit. A wave is
#   sixty-four lanes reading consecutive halves, which is a hundred and
#   twenty-eight contiguous bytes coalesced into two cache lines whatever
#   the per-lane width; widening the lane makes fewer, larger requests for
#   the same bytes, and a kernel with no reuse and nothing to hide behind
#   them is no better for it.
#
#   Nor is it the unit. Replacing the whole activation with an addition --
#   a build that answers wrongly, and only a ceiling -- reads 0.837 s
#   against 0.844: seven milliseconds of the seventy-one, a tenth.
#
#   Which leaves the sixteen gigabytes a second unexplained, and worth
#   recording as a bound rather than a bug: `norm` costs sixty-nine
#   milliseconds and moves about the same, so the two elementwise kernels
#   sit at the same rate. Whatever holds them is shared, is not their
#   arithmetic and is not how wide a lane reads.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-01, the memory kind, and an arithmetic error in the entry above.
#
#   The entry above leaves the device's two elementwise kernels at sixteen
#   gigabytes a second with the access width and the arithmetic both ruled
#   out, and names the memory kind as what was never tested. This machine
#   offers eleven kinds over two heaps, and the ones that matter are three:
#
#     type 3  device-local, host-visible, host-coherent   heap 1, the
#                                                         device's own
#     type 5  host-visible, host-coherent, host-cached    heap 0, not the
#                                                         device's own
#     type 0  device-local and nothing else               heap 1
#
#   The engine allocates uploads and scratch out of type 3, results out of
#   type 5, and nothing at all out of type 0. The argument for trying it:
#   host-coherent is a promise that a processor write is seen without
#   anyone being told, and the way a driver keeps that promise is to stop
#   the device caching the memory -- so a scratch buffer nothing maps might
#   be read through to memory on every access for a guarantee it does not
#   need.
#
#   The half-precision buffer is the one to test: three regions of it,
#   read and written by the tile product and by the combining step, and
#   nothing maps it. Given its own kind, with an Own field on the engine
#   beside Upload and Download:
#
#     type 3, as it is    0.859, 0.845, 0.855 s evaluating the long prompt
#                         1.236, 1.252, 1.234 s generating
#     type 0, its own     0.831, 0.847, 0.848 s
#                         1.254, 1.235, 1.235 s
#
#   Nine tenths of one per cent on the prompt and nothing on the
#   generation, which is the noise. The driver is not giving up the cache
#   for coherence, or the kernels were never waiting on it. Not kept.
#
#   And the entry above overstated the problem it was chasing. Sixteen
#   gigabytes a second counted one dispatch of the combining kernel a
#   layer. There are three: the gated middle, and the two residual joins,
#   which are the same two arms in and one out on a narrower width and go
#   through the same shader. Counted properly a prompt moves about two
#   gigabytes through it rather than one, and the rate is nearer
#   twenty-eight gigabytes a second. That is still under half of what this
#   part should do, but it is not the sixth that made the item worth
#   putting at the top of a list, and the item was ranked on a number this
#   file now corrects.
#
#   So: the access width does not explain it, the arithmetic does not, and
#   the memory kind does not; and the figure it was measured against was
#   nearly twice too small.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-01, a share a worker, decided before any of them started -- and
# the processor's long prompt passes llama.cpp.
#
#   The list said to stop guessing at the generating gap and read what
#   llama.cpp does. Its Q8_0 dot product is the same kernel as this one:
#   one accumulator, eight lanes, a fused multiply-add a block, the sign
#   trick where this has the bias one, and no repacking of Q8_0 weights.
#   The kernels are not the difference.
#
#   The scheduling is. ggml_compute_forward_mul_mat cuts the output rows
#   into chunks of sixteen and hands them out through an atomic counter,
#   falling back to a fixed cut only when there would be fewer than four
#   chunks a thread. This program cut a product into one contiguous range
#   for every worker, worked out before any of them started -- which is
#   right when the workers run at the same speed, and on a fifteen-watt part
#   sharing its boost between eight cores they do not. A job is not done
#   until its slowest range is, and every worker that finished early sat in
#   the spin loop waiting for one that had not.
#
#   So a worker takes its next tile from an atomic counter. The grid is
#   anchored at row zero and a tile is never split, so which task computes a
#   row does not change how the row is summed and the answer is bit for bit
#   what the fixed cut produced -- the property Partition was arranged
#   around and the reason the note above it exists.
#
#     a range a worker   5.412, 5.297, 5.564 s evaluating the long prompt
#                        0.406, 0.417, 0.403 s at 110 tokens
#                        1.919, 1.916, 1.896 s generating
#     a tile at a time   4.781, 4.796, 4.971 s
#                        0.370, 0.355, 0.370 s
#                        1.893, 1.895, 1.886 s
#
#   Eleven per cent of the long prompt, nine of the short one and one of a
#   generated token: the shapes with the most tiles to hand out gain most,
#   and a generated token is one vector wide and already memory-bound.
#
#   Every digest holds at one worker, three, four and seven --
#   33f48397f89839f6 for twelve tokens whatever the worker count -- which is
#   the invariant the fixed cut was arranged around.
#
#   Six groups moved. One sitting, gated to a load under 0.7 and a settled
#   part before the first reading:
#
#     1419-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 5.278 s wall, 4.855 evaluating,
#                                      37.47 s processor, load 0.51

#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.413 s wall, 0.061 and 0.351,

#                                      tg64 38.80 +/- 0.30 and 55.60 +/- 0.01
#
#   The processor reads the long prompt at 292.3 tokens a second against
#   llama.cpp's 268.77 -- one and nine hundredths times faster, where it was
#   level one commit ago and 2.7 times behind when this work began. Seven
#   workers against one reads 5.03x where the sitting before read 4.11x.
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,
#   448c2ed68ec342ee, 3248ac1bb7011de0, 1a26d24d33b8957b, 5f85f06494f88de4,
#   7fbdc947563a2fde, 4b6e8e99ae285b2a.
#
#   The tail of this is worth naming. Three of the last five things tried on
#   the processor were arithmetic and all three were level or refused. This
#   one is not arithmetic at all: the same instructions in the same order,
#   handed out differently. A profile could not have found it either -- it
#   is not a symbol, it is workers idle in a spin loop this file had already
#   decided was earning its keep.
#
# load:  0.51 at the first reading, 4.80 by the last
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0, and Q4_K_M and Q2_K beside it
#
# 2026-09-01, where the device's gap is now, and a bound that was a bug.
#
#   The map is several changes out of date. Taken again -- ours by voiding
#   each shader in turn on the 1419-token prompt, llama.cpp's from
#   GGML_VK_PERF_LOGGER=1 on the same prompt and the same file, summed over
#   its three batches:
#
#     matrix products              595 ms here,  about 575 there   1.03x
#     attention                    108 ms,       80 ms             1.35x
#     the normalization             69 ms,       21 ms             3.3x
#     the gated middle and joins    71 ms,       61 ms (GLU + ADD) 1.16x
#     the rotation                  10 ms,       15 ms (ROPE)      ahead
#
#   The matrix row has caught up. Ours runs at about 4.9 teraflops and
#   theirs at 5.0, where the entry that opened this comparison had ours at
#   3.4 and theirs at 4.9 and put eighty-four per cent of the gap in that
#   one row. The tall-tile arguments stand and the work they were about is
#   done.
#
#   And the entry above this one is wrong where it says the elementwise
#   kernels are at a bound. It ruled out the access width, the arithmetic
#   and the memory kind and concluded the sixteen gigabytes a second was
#   the part rather than the shader. It was the shader: llama.cpp's
#   rms_norm.comp is binary32 and ours was binary64 the whole way through --
#   eight squares a lane, eight folding steps and eight scaled stores, all
#   doubles, on a consumer RDNA3 part that runs double at a sixteenth of
#   single.
#
#   The double was deliberate and the shader's own comment argued for it: a
#   tree fold is a different association from the processor's in-order sum,
#   and binary64 kept the difference in the last bits. But a pairwise fold
#   of two thousand and forty-eight squares carries a relative error of a
#   few parts in ten million and the epsilon added to the mean is a thousand
#   times that.
#
#     binary64   0.838, 0.845, 0.843 s evaluating the long prompt
#                0.100, 0.103, 0.105 s at 110 tokens
#                1.257, 1.237, 1.236 s generating
#     binary32   0.823, 0.832, 0.840 s
#                0.101, 0.100, 0.101 s
#                1.225, 1.241, 1.240 s
#
#   One and three tenths per cent of the long prompt and two of the short
#   one, which is less than the arithmetic suggested: the double was not the
#   whole of that kernel's slowness either. Every digest holds, including
#   cbf29ce484222325, which both backends print for the 110-token prompt.
#   And the shader stops requiring shaderFloat64, which is a device feature
#   it had been asking for on behalf of a sum that never needed it.
#
#   The lane count was swept in the same sitting and is already right: two
#   hundred and fifty-six reads 0.832 s where five hundred and twelve reads
#   0.837 and a hundred and twenty-eight 0.844.
#
#   Two groups moved. One sitting, from a load of 0.26 and a settled part:
#
#     1419-token prompt, 12 generated  cpu 5.040 s wall, 4.632 evaluating,
#                                      36.00 s processor, load 0.26
#                                      device 1.103 s wall, 0.821 evaluating
#     six-token prompt, 12 generated   cpu 0.393 s wall, 0.053 and 0.340,
#                                      1.76 s processor
#                                      device 0.251 s wall, 0.033 and 0.217,
#                                      0.07 s processor

#                                      device 0.095 s (0.094), 0.01 s
#     64 generated, six-token prompt   cpu 1.895 s (1.842), device 1.243 s

#   Both processor prompt rows are ahead now -- 306.4 tokens a second
#   against 272.5 at 1419 and 314.3 against 311.0 at 110 -- and the device's
#   long prompt is 1.05 behind, its closest yet. llama.cpp read faster in
#   this sitting than in the one before it on every row; the machine was
#   quieter and both sides moved together, which is what a comparison taken
#   in one sitting is for.
#
#   Digests held: 33f48397f89839f6, 5abff916f9d83ca6, cbf29ce484222325,

# load:  0.26 to 1.77 across the sitting
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the normalization taken apart, and nothing kept.
#
#   The item this came from said the normalization was 2.8 times behind and
#   worth thirty-seven milliseconds, and named the eight barriers of its
#   fold as the mechanism. Both halves of that were wrong, and the number
#   was stale: it came from a removal sweep taken several commits before the
#   binary32 change, and the kernel has been re-measured since.
#
#   Taken apart on the long prompt, each part voided in turn against a
#   0.830-second baseline:
#
#     as it is                                   0.830 s
#     the sum pass gone, a constant gain         0.834
#     the output pass gone                       0.794
#     neither pass                               0.795
#
#   So the whole kernel is thirty-five milliseconds, not fifty-eight, and
#   llama.cpp's is twenty-one: 1.7 times behind and fourteen milliseconds of
#   an 830-millisecond prompt. Not the largest thing left on that side.
#
#   And the fold is not any of it. Eight halvings is eight barriers across
#   four waves; folded in two turns instead -- thirty-two partials to each
#   of eight lanes, then those eight -- it reads 0.835 against 0.830. The
#   barriers were free, which is why the subgroup reduction that item was
#   really proposing was not built.
#
#   The sum pass is free too, which the table above says plainly: removing
#   the first read of the row and the squares makes the kernel no faster.
#   Everything is in the output pass -- and inside that pass the reads are
#   free as well. Writing a constant, reading neither the row nor the gain,
#   still reads 0.832.
#
#   So the whole cost of this kernel is its store. And the store is not
#   slow for its width: a binary32 store of twice the bytes reads 0.819,
#   eleven milliseconds faster than the half-precision one -- but that
#   variant writes the result buffer rather than the half-precision buffer,
#   and packing two halves into one four-byte store of the same buffer
#   reads 0.838. It is not the width. It is which buffer is written.
#
#   Which points somewhere this file has already looked and not found
#   anything: the result buffer is host-cached memory out of the heap that
#   is not the device's own, and the half buffer is the device's own and
#   host-coherent. The entry two above this one moved the half buffer to a
#   kind that is the device's own and nothing else and measured nothing.
#   That was the right idea tested at the wrong end -- it looked for a
#   faster read where the cost is a write -- and it is the open question
#   this leaves.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the open question above, asked the other way and still no.
#
#   The entry above ends with a buffer written by the normalization costing
#   eleven milliseconds more than a buffer written by nothing else, and
#   names the memory kind as the thing to try -- this time for the write
#   rather than the read.
#
#   Both ends were tried. Host-cached memory out of the heap that is not the
#   device's own, which is what the result buffer uses and what was eleven
#   milliseconds faster to write:
#
#     as it is                   0.830 s evaluating the long prompt
#     the half buffer cached     0.860
#
#   Thirty milliseconds worse, because the tile product reads that buffer on
#   every dispatch and a read out of that heap is what this file already
#   knew to be slow. Fast to write and slow to read is not a trade a buffer
#   read far more than it is written can take.
#
#   And the other end, the kind that is the device's own and nothing else --
#   no host bits, so no coherence to keep and nothing forcing a write past a
#   cache. Four rounds this time rather than three:
#
#     host-visible and coherent  0.832, 0.839, 0.829, 0.825 s
#     the device's own alone     0.819, 0.830, 0.823, 0.836
#
#   Half a per cent, with the arms overlapping at both ends. The same answer
#   the read-side test gave, on four rounds instead of three, and the same
#   verdict.
#
#   So the eleven milliseconds is neither the heap, nor the property bits,
#   nor the width of the store, nor what is in it. What is left untried is
#   the one thing the two variants also differ in: the half-precision buffer
#   is written by the normalization, read by the tile product, written by
#   the tile product and read by the combining step, all inside one
#   submission -- so every barrier in a layer is a barrier over that buffer.
#   The result buffer, in the variant that was faster, was touched by
#   nothing else in the sequence. That is the next thing to ask, and it is
#   about barriers rather than about memory.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, what the barriers cost, which bounds the question above.
#
#   A layer's steps are a chain -- normalize, three projections, rotate,
#   attend, project out, join, normalize, two arms, combine, project down,
#   join -- and the engine walks a memory barrier in wherever a step reads
#   what a step before it wrote. About ten a layer, twenty-two layers, three
#   batches: something over six hundred in a 1419-token prompt.
#
#   Priced by removing them, which answers wrongly and is only a ceiling:
#
#     as it is                         0.830 s evaluating the long prompt
#                                      1.225 s generating
#     no barriers at all               0.809
#                                      1.199
#
#   Twenty-one milliseconds of eight hundred and thirty, which is two and a
#   half per cent and the whole budget for anything barrier-shaped. The
#   eleven milliseconds the entry above is chasing is half of that, and it
#   cannot be the count: both of those variants issue the same barriers in
#   the same places.
#
#   Split, by asking for one that orders execution and asks for no cache
#   work -- no memory barrier in the call at all, which is not correct and
#   is only a measurement:
#
#     write-to-read over all memory    0.830 s
#     execution only                   0.816
#
#   So seven milliseconds is the drain -- a dispatch waiting for the one
#   before it to empty, which is what a chain of dependent steps is -- and
#   fourteen is the cache work. That fourteen is what a shader read after a
#   shader write requires on this part: the per-unit vector caches have to be
#   invalidated or a compute unit may read a line another wrote. It answered
#   correctly without them in this run, which is luck about which lines
#   collided and not a property to keep.
#
#   Nothing here is removable, and the ceiling is what the entry is worth
#   writing for: two and a half per cent, most of it required. The eleven
#   milliseconds remains unexplained, but it is now bounded by a number
#   small enough that it is not the next thing to chase.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-02, attention read line by line against llama.cpp's, and nothing
# to take from it.
#
#   Attention is a hundred and eight milliseconds of a device prompt against
#   llama.cpp's eighty -- 1.35 times, twenty-eight milliseconds, and the
#   largest named item left on that side. So flash_attn_cm1.comp was read
#   through rather than sampled, beside this kernel.
#
#   They are the same design, step for step:
#
#     the scores            a cooperative matrix product of the queries
#                           against a tile of keys, stored to shared memory
#     the softmax           read back into ordinary registers, the running
#                           maximum and total kept there, everything
#                           accumulated so far rescaled by the exponential
#                           of the change in maximum
#     the weighing          a second cooperative matrix product, its result
#                           stored to shared memory and added into those
#                           same registers
#
#   That last step is the one this file thought might be theirs alone --
#   keeping the output in registers so it can be rescaled, because a
#   cooperative matrix cannot be. It is not: coopMatStore into pvsh and
#   Of[r][d] += pvsh[...] is exactly what this kernel does with pacc and ss.
#   The tile is the same too, sixteen query rows by sixty-four cached
#   positions, which get_fa_tuning_params_coopmat1 gives this device and
#   which this kernel already used.
#
#   The one difference is the four subgroups, and that was built two entries
#   below this one and is fourteen per cent slower here.
#
#   The one parameter left unswept is the query block, and it is at its
#   peak. It must be a multiple of sixteen for the instruction, so there are
#   two candidates:
#
#     sixteen query rows    0.830 s evaluating the long prompt
#     thirty-two           0.911
#
#   Ten per cent worse, the same answer every taller tile on this device has
#   given. Sixteen it is.
#
#   So the twenty-eight milliseconds is not the tile, not the structure, not
#   the split and not the block. Reading the other runtime's source has
#   twice now been the thing that found an answer on the processor side and
#   twice now found nothing here, which is worth recording as a result about
#   the two sides rather than about attention: this program's device kernels
#   are shaped the way the other runtime's are, and where they differ it is
#   because this part measured against the difference.
#

#
#   The chunk grain, which the pool takes a worker's next piece from. It has
#   never been swept: it was set to the row tile because that is what the
#   tiling requires as a floor, and the floor was never tested against

#   a large matrix, which is a deliberate two-value choice in

#     four tiles, 128       5.589, 5.544    1.872, 1.873    0.417, 0.444
#
#   One tile is the best of them and the curve is monotone: two costs four
#   per cent of the long prompt, three eight and four fifteen. The floor is
#   also the optimum, which is luck rather than design -- the grain cannot
#   go below a tile without splitting one, and splitting one would change
#   which kernel a row's tile takes and move the answers at different worker
#   counts. So the constant is right and the reason it is right is not the
#   reason it was chosen.

#   keeps its elementwise loops on the calling task; that was measured when

#   has so little elementwise work that neither sharing it nor keeping it

#   about them, which is exactly what the constant was put there to say.

# 2026-09-02, the short device prompt: the premise refuted, and the other
# runtime's tile table read.
#
#   The device's 110-token prompt is 1.53 times behind, the widest ratio in
#   this file. The reason offered was occupancy: a 110-column batch is one
#   column tile wide, so a 2048-row product dispatches sixty-four workgroups
#   onto twelve compute units and the tail is a large part of the whole. A
#   narrower column tile would double the count.
#
#     a hundred and twenty-eight columns   0.101 s at 110 tokens
#                                          0.841 s at 1419
#     sixty-four                           0.103
#                                          0.846
#
#   Nothing at 110 and slightly worse at 1419, with twice the workgroups.
#   That is the fourth independent measurement on this device to say
#   occupancy is not the constraint, after the taller product tile, the
#   staged operands and the four-subgroup attention.

#   575 and 4073 gigaflops for the four products a layer makes; at n=512
#   they read 4552, 5581, 2097 and 5002. So they lose between a quarter and
#   a third going from five hundred and twelve columns to a hundred and ten,
#   and we lose forty-seven per cent -- 0.80 milliseconds a token against
#   0.425. The narrow one, the 256-row key and value projection, is the

# 2026-09-02, the step's depth -- and a correction: it had been done.
#
#   The entry above ends by calling the depth "the one dimension of the tile
#   this program has never varied" and "the only untried thing left in this

#   I edited. The shader says, of TILE_R, TILE_V and KCH: "The step is one
#   Q8_0 block deep, which is what makes the decode below a whole number of

#   twenty-eight 2.122. The rewrite cost one and a half per cent and was
#   taken out, and a repository check was added that TILE_R and KCH are
#   thirty-two so that the next person to turn one gets a failure rather
#   than a kernel that runs and answers nothing.
#
#   That check exists and it names this exact mistake. It did not fire
#   because I never ran it: the sweep went through the shader build script
#   and the speed tool, and the repository check lives in `tests check`,
#   which I skipped for being slow. The guard was right, it was written for
#   me, and I walked round it.
#
#   What the date rule does justify is re-asking, because that sweep was
#   taken when the long device prompt was 1.935 seconds and it is now 0.831
#   -- the shape was chosen on a machine doing less than half the work per

#   the 2026-08-29 rewrite: the staging keeps its straight-line body and

#   And the loop costs four and a half per cent where the earlier and more

# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0

# 2026-09-02, the weights' traffic costs nothing, and the normalization's
# store costs more than it did.
#
#   Two questions, and the first overturns what this file has said about the
#   generating row since the counters were first read.
#
#   Generating reads every weight once a token -- 1.17 gigabytes -- and the
#   standing explanation of the gap to llama.cpp has been that the bus
#   answers rather than the arithmetic. Four hypotheses about that bus have
#   been refused here: the page tables, the accumulator chain, the
#   prefetcher and the memory kind. So the traffic itself was removed
#   instead. The insertion's weight cursor is left at zero, so every block
#   of every row reads the same thirty-two bytes and the same instructions
#   run in the same order on data that is always in the nearest cache. The
#   answers are wrong and finite, which is all a timing needs.
#
#     five shares, as it is        1.800 s generating, 9.29 s of processor
#     five shares, one block       1.804,               9.32
#     one worker, as it is         4.553
#     one worker, one block        4.327
#
#   A gigabyte and a sixth a token, and taking it away is worth nothing at
#   the share count this program uses and five per cent at one worker.
#   Whatever the generating row is waiting for at five shares, it is not the
#   weights arriving.
#
#   That does not say what it is. The arithmetic does not add up either: the
#   kernel is 79 per cent of the samples and about 380 million instructions
#   a token, which at five cores and this part's all-core clock is a
#   quarter of the twenty-eight milliseconds a token takes; the dependent
#   multiply-add chain is another quarter. Something is being waited for
#   that is neither the weights, nor the issue rate, nor the chain, and this
#   entry does not find it. What it does is take the bus off the list, which
#   four earlier entries assumed and none of them tested.
#
#   The second question is the normalization's store, which the entry two
#   above this one left as the one unexplained number on the device side.
#   The probe was run again on the build that has it in binary32:
#
#     writing the half-precision copy   0.828, 0.831 s evaluating the long
#                                       prompt
#     writing the binary32 one          0.800
#
#   Thirty-one milliseconds, where the same probe read eleven before the
#   binary32 change -- so the number grew as the kernel around it shrank,
#   and it is now nearly four per cent of a device prompt. The probe is not
#   a change: the tile product's operand must be half precision and the
#   binary32 copy is what the conversion kernel used to make. It is a
#   ceiling and a puzzle, and the list of things it is not is unchanged:
#   not the heap, not the property bits, not the width of the store, not
#   what is in it, not the barriers.
#
#   One thing that has not been tried and fits every reading: the
#   half-precision buffer is three regions of five and four fifths
#   megabytes, seventeen in all, where the result buffer is four and a
#   fifth. Which of them a kernel writes changes what is resident across the
#   dispatches either side of it, and this part's second-level cache is two
#   megabytes. That is a size argument rather than a kind argument, and it
#   is the next thing to ask.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the size argument, and what the instruction turns out to be.
#
#   The entry above proposes that the normalization's store is dearer into
#   the half-precision buffer than into the result buffer because the first
#   is seventeen megabytes and the second four, against a second-level cache
#   of two. The batch is what sets that size -- the region is the widest
#   product's columns times the batch rounded to a tile -- so the batch was
#   cut to a quarter and the same probe run at both:
#
#     batch 512, writing the halves    0.848 s evaluating the long prompt
#     batch 512, writing binary32      0.819
#     batch 128, writing the halves    0.917
#     batch 128, writing binary32      0.890
#
#   Seventeen megabytes against four and a third, a fourfold cut in the
#   working set, and the gap is twenty-nine milliseconds against
#   twenty-seven. It is not the size.
#
#   So the instruction was read rather than guessed at. RADV_DEBUG=asm on a
#   device run prints every kernel it compiles; the normalization is the
#   short one with a square root in it.
#
#     writing h[at + c]                buffer_store_b16
#     writing y[at + c]                buffer_store_b32
#     writing h2[(at + c) / 2]         buffer_store_b32
#
#   Two things follow. The half store is a real sixteen-bit store and not a
#   read-modify-write of the word around it, which was the last mechanism
#   that would have explained a per-element penalty. And the pair-packed
#   variant really did emit the wide store -- so when it measured 0.838
#   against 0.830 it was a wide store into the half buffer losing to a
#   narrow one into the same buffer, not a failed compilation.
#
#   Which leaves the destination and nothing else. A thirty-two bit store
#   into the result buffer is faster than a sixteen-bit store into the half
#   buffer, and a thirty-two bit store into the half buffer is slower than
#   either. Six things have now been tried and none of them is it: the
#   memory kind at both ends, the size of the buffer, the width of the
#   store, what is in it, and the barriers -- and the barriers are bounded
#   at twenty-one milliseconds for a whole prompt, which is less than the
#   twenty-nine this costs.
#
#   The difference that is left is what else touches the buffer inside a
#   submission. The half-precision buffer is written by the normalization,
#   read by the tile product, written by the tile product and read by the
#   combining step; the result buffer, in the faster variant, is touched by
#   nothing else in the sequence. That was named two entries ago as a
#   barrier question and the barrier budget rules it out as one, so
#   whatever it is, it is not something this file has a name for yet.
#
#   Recorded as an open question with six doors closed, which is worth more
#   than the seventh guess.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the probe behind three entries was invalid, and there is no
# thirty-one milliseconds.
#
#   Three entries have chased a store: the normalization writes its answer
#   into the half-precision buffer, and writing it into the result buffer
#   instead reads twenty-nine to thirty-one milliseconds faster. Six
#   explanations have been eliminated -- the memory kind at both ends, the
#   size of the buffer, the width of the store, what is in it, the barriers.
#   The seventh test was of the probe itself, and it is the one that should
#   have been done first.
#
#   First, is the effect general? The combining step writes the same buffer
#   the same way. Given the same treatment -- its answer to the result
#   buffer instead -- the long device prompt reads 0.829 s against a
#   baseline of 0.828 to 0.848. Nothing, where the same change to the
#   normalization is worth twenty-nine milliseconds.
#
#   Second, what does the write actually cost? Made to write both -- the
#   halves where they belong and the binary32 copy beside them, which is a
#   correct build answering 1a26d24d33b8957b -- it reads 0.838. So adding a
#   4.2-megabyte binary32 store to that kernel costs about five
#   milliseconds, where removing a 2.1-megabyte half store is supposed to
#   save twenty-nine.
#
#   Third, and this is the answer. In the probe, nothing writes the
#   normalization's region of the half buffer at all, and the three
#   projections and the two arms read it. So the region was changed to be
#   written once a row -- five hundred and twelve stores of two bytes rather
#   than a million, every page of it touched and almost nothing put in it:
#
#     as it is, the whole region written    0.838 s
#     one store a row, and the binary32     0.847
#     no store at all, and the binary32     0.810
#
#   Writing a megabyte into the region and writing a kilobyte into it cost
#   the same. Writing nothing into it is what is cheap. **The twenty-nine
#   milliseconds is not the store; it is the reads downstream of a region
#   that has been written, against reads of one that has not.** The probe
#   replaced a real dependency with reads of memory nobody had touched, and
#   what it measured was the normalization's output existing.
#
#   Which also explains the combining step's result: its region is written
#   by the tile products as well, so skipping its own write leaves the
#   region dirty and there is nothing to save.
#
#   So there is no thirty-one milliseconds and there never was. The entries
#   above that call it unexplained were explaining an artefact, and the six
#   doors they closed were closed on an empty room. What is worth keeping is
#   the rule: a probe that removes a write must be asked what reads it, and
#   a saving that appears when a buffer stops being written is a saving in
#   the readers.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, what a voided kernel really measures, and the budget table it
# puts in doubt.
#
#   The rule the entry above ends on -- a probe that removes a write must be
#   asked what reads it -- applies to this file's main instrument. Every
#   device budget here was taken by voiding a kernel and reading the whole
#   run, and every kernel in that budget writes something another one reads.
#
#   So the normalization was measured three ways rather than two:
#
#     as it is                          0.833 s evaluating the long prompt
#     one store a row and nothing else  0.829
#     voided entirely                   0.783
#
#   The middle build keeps the kernel's shape and throws away its work: the
#   sum, the fold, the gain, and a million stores become five hundred and
#   twelve stores of two bytes, one to a row, which touches every page of
#   the region and puts almost nothing in it.
#
#   Four milliseconds is what the normalization does. Forty-six is what its
#   output costs the five kernels that read it -- three projections and two
#   arms -- against reading a region nobody wrote. The removal method
#   charges the kernel for both and reports fifty.
#
#   Which is a correction to every figure in `### Where a device prompt
#   goes, every kernel taken out in turn` and to the budget under `### Where
#   the device's gap is now`. Those tables say what removing a kernel saves.
#   They do not say what the kernel costs, and for this one the two differ
#   by a factor of twelve.
#
#   It also explains a thing this file recorded as unexplained. At a
#   110-token prompt each of five small kernels appeared to cost about
#   thirty-two milliseconds alone and all five together thirty-five, which
#   could not be true of any of them. It is true of none of them: most of
#   each figure is the same downstream reads becoming cheap, and removing a
#   second kernel cannot make them cheap twice.
#
#   What survives is the two ends of such a table -- everything, and nothing
#   -- which are both real, and the ordering, which is roughly right because
#   a kernel whose output is read more has more readers to make cheap. What
#   does not survive is the share.
#
#   The middle build is the instrument to use from here: keep the writes,
#   throw away the work. It is more trouble than an early return and it is
#   the only one of the two that measures a kernel.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the device budget taken again with the instrument that
# measures a kernel, and the map is not what it was.
#
#   Every share in the two budget tables was a removal figure, and the entry
#   above shows a removal figure is the kernel's work plus what its output
#   costs its readers. So the budget was taken again: each kernel keeps its
#   stores and loses its work, and the difference from the whole run is the
#   kernel.
#
#   Against a 0.841-second long device prompt:
#
#     matrix_product, the product loop not run    0.326 s   515 ms is it
#     attention, one key tile instead of all      0.735     106
#     combine, the unit dropped                   0.802      39
#     norm, the sum dropped                       0.840        1
#       and its output pass, from the entry above                4
#     rotate, voided                              0.826      15 at most
#
#   Beside what removal used to say -- matrix_product 595, attention 108,
#   combine 71, norm 69, rotate 10 -- two of the five are transformed. The
#   normalization is five milliseconds and was reported as sixty-nine. The
#   combining step is thirty-nine and was seventy-one. Attention is
#   unchanged at a hundred and six, which is right: its output is written in
#   both arms, so there was never a reader artefact in it.
#
#   And the kernels no longer add up to the prompt. Five hundred and fifteen,
#   a hundred and six, thirty-nine, five and fifteen is six hundred and
#   eighty of eight hundred and forty-one. **A hundred and sixty
#   milliseconds -- one fifth of a device prompt -- is not any kernel's
#   work.** Twenty-one of it is the barriers, which were priced separately.
#   The rest is between the kernels rather than in them.
#
#   Which inverts the comparison this file has been drawing. llama.cpp's own
#   logger reports kernel durations, which are own-work figures; at 1419
#   tokens it reads about 575 milliseconds of matrix products, 80 of
#   attention, 61 of the gated middle and the joins, 21 of normalization and
#   15 of rotation. Kernel for kernel:
#
#     matrix products     515 here against about 575    ahead
#     attention           106 against 80                1.33 behind
#     the gated middle     39 against 61                ahead
#     the normalization      5 against 21               far ahead
#     the rotation          15 against 15               level
#
#   Six hundred and eighty against seven hundred and fifty-two. **This
#   program's device kernels are faster than the other runtime's, in total,
#   and its device prompt is slower.** The difference is the fifth of the
#   prompt that belongs to no kernel.
#
#   That is a different thing from everything this file has chased on the
#   device side. The tall tile, the staged operands, the four-subgroup
#   attention, the narrow column tile and the deeper step were all judged
#   against a map that said the matrix row was most of the prompt. It is
#   sixty-one per cent of it, and the kernels together are eighty.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped -- but the
#   shares in the two budget tables are superseded by this one and the
#   README says so where they stand.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the fifth that is in no kernel, found -- and the comparison in
# the entry above withdrawn.
#
#   Three builds, one long device prompt:
#
#     nothing changed                              0.833 s
#     every kernel keeps its stores, loses its
#       work                                       0.166
#     every kernel voided                          0.043
#
#   Which decomposes the prompt without any of it left over:
#
#     the kernels' arithmetic and their reads      667 ms   80 per cent
#     their stores and what those cost             123      15
#     the host, the submissions and the launches    43       5
#
#   The first figure is also a check on the instrument: the five kernels
#   measured one at a time came to six hundred and eighty, and measured all
#   at once they come to six hundred and sixty-seven. Two per cent apart, so
#   the own-work figures do add.
#
#   The hundred and twenty-three is the fifth this was sent to find, and it
#   is not one thing that can be pointed at: in that build every kernel
#   writes everything it would write and computes nothing, so what is left
#   is the stores themselves, the memory traffic they cause, and every
#   downstream read of what they wrote. The normalization's three-way
#   measurement says which of those it mostly is -- writing a megabyte into
#   a region and writing a kilobyte into it cost the same, and writing
#   nothing is what is cheap -- so it is the reads, not the volume stored.
#
#   The forty-three is the floor: the host, the submissions and about eight
#   hundred dispatch launches, which is fifty microseconds apiece. At a
#   110-token prompt the same build reads eight milliseconds, and a third of
#   the batches is about a third of the dispatches.
#
#   AND THE WITHDRAWAL. The entry above puts this program's device kernels
#   at six hundred and eighty milliseconds against llama.cpp's seven hundred
#   and fifty-two and says ours are faster. That is a category error and the
#   claim does not stand. llama.cpp's logger measures a dispatch from
#   timestamp to timestamp, so its figures are durations and include every
#   memory effect inside them. Ours are differences of whole-run times with
#   a kernel's work dropped, so they exclude the hundred and twenty-three
#   milliseconds that this kernel's output costs elsewhere. The two columns
#   are not the same quantity and should never have been put beside each
#   other.
#
#   What can be said is the whole-prompt figure, which is what this file
#   publishes anyway: 0.833 against 0.787, six per cent, and the gap of
#   1.07. The three-way split above is a map of this program's own time and
#   nothing about the other runtime follows from it.
#
#   Nothing changed, so no figure moves and no group is restamped.
#
# load:  under 1.05 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, both short-prompt rows were measured on a machine that had gone
# back to sleep, and both are wrong. A prompt of 110 tokens is a tenth of a
# second of work, and `tests speed` takes the median of three of them with the
# host idle in between. What the fixture behind those two rows does is stop
# the model immediately -- it is a complete instruction and the chat model
# answers it with an end of sequence -- so the run generates nothing, nothing
# follows the prefill, and the parts never leave their low state.
#
#   Sampling /sys/class/drm/card1/device/pp_dpm_sclk through both:
#
#     a run that generates nothing    800 MHz, reaching 1.1 to 1.8 GHz
#     a run that generates sixty-four 800 MHz idle, holding 2.2, peak 2.49
#
#   The same prompt length, half the clock. And it is not only the device: the
#   processor's short row moves by nine per cent for the same reason.
#
#   A second fixture, speed-prompt-110.txt: the same prose, 110 tokens of it,
#   in one line rather than eight so the model continues rather than stopping.
#   Alternated against the old one, three rounds, load under 0.70 before each:
#
#     device, generating 64   0.071  0.072  0.068
#     device, generating 0    0.103  0.102  0.100
#     processor, generating 64 0.330 0.330  0.311
#     processor, generating 0  0.360 0.362  0.348
#
#   Which moves both published rows:
#
#                     was        is        llama.cpp   was     is
#     device 110    1078.4    1549.3 t/s     1647.2    1.53    1.06
#     processor 110  296.5     333.3         339.5     1.15    1.02
#
#   The widest gap in this file was a clock state. llama-bench warms up and
#   then runs its repeats back to back, so its short-prompt figure was taken
#   on parts at speed and ours was not; the two rows were never measuring the
#   same machine.
#
#   The 1419-token rows are not affected and were checked rather than assumed:
#   that fixture generates twelve tokens and its prompt is eight tenths of a
#   second of work, which is long enough to bring the clock up inside itself.
#   It reads 0.824 s here against the 0.841 published, which is noise.
#
#   What this is an instance of: every figure in this file is taken by a tool
#   that measures a tenth of a second and then stops. The machine is a
#   participant, and a run too short to wake it measures the sleeping one. The
#   fixture that does this has been behind the short rows since they were
#   first published, and what made it visible was not suspicion of the number
#   but the fact that runs generating twelve tokens and runs generating none
#   sorted into two clean bands with no overlap.
#
#   No source changed, so no group is restamped. The new fixture is a fixture,
#   not a source of any figure group.
#
# load:  under 0.70 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the residual joins folded into the products they follow.
#
#   What they cost, by removal, on the 1419-token device prompt:
#
#     nothing changed                              0.826 s
#     the join stops reading the residual          0.834
#     the join voided entirely                     0.772
#
#   Fifty-four milliseconds, six and a half per cent of the prompt, and none
#   of it is the reading: dropping one of the two arms changed nothing a
#   measurement can see. It is the dispatch and the store, and what that
#   store costs whoever reads it -- the same shape the normalization's three
#   -way measurement found, where a megabyte written and a kilobyte written
#   cost the same and writing nothing was what was cheap.
#
#   A layer joins twice: once to add its input back to what attention made,
#   once to add it back to what the feed-forward made. Each was a dispatch of
#   its own -- two arms read, a buffer written, and the normalization after
#   it reading that buffer back. Both follow a matrix product immediately,
#   and nothing else reads that product on its own, so the product stores the
#   sum instead and the join is not dispatched at all.
#
#   The join's step stays in the sequence. A caller writes step numbers --
#   `From_Step => 16, Residual_Step => 11` -- and a step that vanished would
#   move every number after it, so the step stays, is marked folded, and its
#   place is the product's place. A later step naming the join reads what the
#   product wrote.
#
#   Measured, three alternated rounds, load under 0.80 before each:
#
#     1419-token prompt   0.818 s against 0.841   2.7 per cent
#     64 tokens generated 1.263 s against 1.280   1.3 per cent
#
#   Twenty-three of the fifty-four. The rest is the store itself, which the
#   product still makes -- what the fold removes is a dispatch and a buffer,
#   not a write.
#
#   Every digest is unchanged: cbf29ce484222325 at 110 tokens,
#   1a26d24d33b8957b at 1419, 448c2ed68ec342ee generating, and each of them
#   equal to what the processor produces for the same prompt.
#
#   AND THE TRAP, which is worth more than the two per cent. A layer's last
#   step leaves its answer at the front of the result buffer for the next
#   sequence to read, and the room reserved there was the activation's own
#   size. That was right while the last step was a join, because a join
#   writes the columns the batch really holds. A product on the matrix kernel
#   writes the columns the rounding invented as well -- a hundred and
#   twenty-eight where the batch holds a hundred and ten -- so the folded
#   product's store did not fit, the carry was refused rather than trimmed,
#   and the sequence after it read a room nothing had written.
#
#   What named it was the shape of the wrongness: every batch was wrong
#   except one of exactly 128 tokens, which is a whole tile and has no
#   invented columns. A single prompt at that length is what turned an
#   arithmetic suspicion into a rounding one. The room is now the wider of
#   the activation and a whole tile's store.
#
# load:  under 0.80 before every reading
# model: TinyLlama-1.1B-Chat Q8_0
#
# 2026-09-02, the sitting the fold's figures were taken in. Load under 0.70
# before every reading, medians of three, llama-bench -r 3 in the same
# sitting.
#
#   tests speed:
#
#     6-token prompt, 12 generated   processor 0.392 s wall, 0.052
#                                    evaluating, 0.340 generating, 2.06 s of
#                                    processor time
#                                    device 0.262, 0.033, 0.229, 0.08
#     110 tokens, nothing generated  processor 0.354 s, 2.74 s of processor
#                                    time; device 0.103, 0.02
#     110 tokens, 64 generated       processor 0.310 s and 1.841 s
#                                    device 0.070 and 1.247
#     1419 tokens                    processor 4.853 s; device 0.822
#     6 tokens, 64 generated         processor 1.817 s; device 1.238
#
#   llama-bench, same model, same host, llama.cpp at 95b8e33e1:
#
#     --device none  pp110 346.84 +/- 34.45   tg64 39.13
#     -ngl 99        pp110 1579.08 +/- 44.82  tg64 56.65
#     --device none  pp1419 262.86            tg64 38.79
#     -ngl 99        pp1419 1823.16           tg64 56.32
#     -ngl 0         pp110 764.25, which is the figure that says why the
#                    command names --device none: with a device present and
#                    no layers offloaded llama.cpp still reads the prompt on
#                    it, and a reader who takes this the obvious way measures
#                    the device and writes it down as the processor.
#
#   Which gives, at 110 tokens: processor 354.8 t/s against 346.84, ahead;
#   device 1571.4 against 1579.08, level within llama-bench's own spread.
#   At 1419: processor 292.4 against 262.86, ahead by 1.11; device 1726.3
#   against 1823.16, 1.06 behind. Generating: processor 35.2 against 39.13,
#   1.11 behind; device 51.7 against 56.65, 1.10 behind.
#
#   Two rows changed character rather than value. The 110-token processor row
#   is ahead for the first time, and the 110-token device row is level -- and
#   most of that is the entry above about the clock rather than the fold.
#   llama-bench's own pp110 carries a spread of ten per cent here, so "level"
#   is the honest word for the device row and "ahead" for the processor one
#   is inside a spread of its own.
#
# 2026-09-02, the processor's generating row, and the two things it is not.
#
#   Where it goes. A profile of sixty-four generated tokens puts 77.8 per
#   cent of the samples in the byte row kernel and 10.1 in the worker's spin,
#   which says nothing about why, so the question was asked of the bus
#   instead:
#
#     one run alone      38.6 GB/s of the model
#     two runs at once   43.1 GB/s between them
#
#   Twelve per cent the part will deliver and one run was not asking for.
#   llama.cpp alone reads 42.6, which is the saturated figure, so the whole
#   of this row's gap is that headroom.
#
#   TWO THINGS IT IS NOT, both built and both measured flat. Neither is kept.
#
#     one row at a time         1.808 s for 64 tokens
#     two rows at a time        1.810
#     two rows and a prefetch   1.812
#
#   The row kernel walks one row with a multiply-add chain between each load
#   and the next, so two rows at once are two streams of loads a worker that
#   do not wait for each other -- nine instructions a row-block against
#   eleven, and each row still summing its own blocks in its own accumulator
#   in the same order, so no digest moves. It bought nothing, and a software
#   prefetch three hundred and twenty bytes ahead of each stream bought
#   nothing either. Whatever the twelve per cent is, it is not memory-level
#   parallelism inside a worker.
#
#   WHERE IT ACTUALLY IS. The engine's own phase account, which `tests speed
#   --budget` prints and which nothing here had read for this question:
#
#     feeding        1.256 s   69.6 per cent
#     projecting     0.358     19.8
#     reading out    0.102      5.7
#     attending      0.049      2.7
#     normalizing, rotating, joining  0.040   2.2
#
#   Against each phase's share of the model's bytes -- 761 M parameters in
#   the feed-forward, 207 M in the projections, 65 M in the vocabulary, of
#   1034 M -- those are 73.6, 20.0 and 6.3 per cent. So the products are
#   proportional to their bytes and the rest, four and nine tenths per cent,
#   is work that reads almost nothing. And per phase:
#
#     feeding      41.2 GB/s
#     projecting   39.3
#     reading out  43.7
#
#   The one big product of the vocabulary reaches the saturated figure; the
#   feed-forward nearly does; the projections, which are the smallest
#   matrices and the most jobs, do not.
#
#   WHAT IS KEPT. A group of matrices reading one input is one job of the
#   pool rather than one each. A layer's queries, keys and values are one
#   job and its gate and up another, so a generated token posts eighty-nine
#   jobs where it posted a hundred and fifty-five. The parts share the chunk
#   counter but not their tiles -- each part's tiles begin at a tile
#   boundary of its own -- so a part's rows are cut where they would have
#   been cut alone and no answer moves.
#
#   Alternated, three rounds, load under 0.75 before each, medians of three:
#
#     projecting    0.363 s against 0.387    6.2 per cent
#     feeding       1.267    against 1.276   0.7
#     generating    1.787    against 1.808   1.2
#
#   Which is the phase the change is aimed at moving by six per cent and the
#   run moving by one, because the phase is a fifth of the run. Every digest
#   is unchanged.
#
